ia



1. This Report deals with the results of audit of Government companies
and Statutory corporations which has been conducted under Section 19A of
the Comptroller and Auditor General's (Duties, Powers and Conditions of
Service) Act, 1971, as amended from time to time. The results of audit
relating to departmentally managed commercial undertakings are presented
separately

2. Audit of the accounts of Government companies is conducted by CAG
under the provisions of Section 619 of the Companies Act, 1956. Audit of
Statutory Corporations of the State is governed by respective legislation.

3. The cases mentioned in this Report are those which came to notice in
the course of audit during the year 2012-2013 as well as those which came to
notice in earlier years, but were not included in the previous Reports. Matters
relating to the period subsequent to 2012-2013 have also been included,
wherever necessary.

4. The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing
Standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.



Overview

[1. Overview of Government Companies and Statutory Corporations

Audit of Government Companies is governed by Section 619 of the Companies Act 1956.
The accounts of Government Companies are audited hy Statutory Auditors appointed by
CAG. These accounts are also subject to supplementary audit conducted by CAG. Audit of
Statutory corporation is governed by their respective legislations. As on 31 March 2013, the
State of Madhya Pradesh had 55 working PSUs (52 Companies and 3 Statutory
corporations) and 9 non-working PSUs (all Companies), which has employed 58459
employees.

Investments in PSUs

As on 31 March 2013, the investment (Capital and Long Term Loans) in 65 PSUs was
& 46365.94 crore. It grew by 181.47 per cent from © 16472.66 crore in 2007-08. Power
Sector accounted for 93.30 per cent of total investment in 2012-13. The State Government
contributed % 12156.08 crore towards Equity, Loans and Grants/Subsidies to State PSUs
during 2012-13.

Performance of PSUs

During the year 2012-13, out of 55 working PSUs, 26 PSUs earned profit of €275.94 crore
Seven PSUs neither earned profit nor incurred losses and 18 PSUs incurred loss of
% 4307.57 crore as per their latest financial accounts as on 30 September 2013. Four PSUs
did not submit their first accounts. The losses are attributable to various deficiencies in the
functioning of PSUs. A review of three years’ Audit Reports of CAG shows that the State
PSUs’ losses of € 458.22 crore and in fructuous investments of %62.97 crore which could
have been controlled with better management.

Comments on accounts

The quality of accounts of PSUs needs improvement. All 49 accounts of working PSUs
finalised during October 2012 to September 2013 received qualified certificates from
Statutory Auditors. There were 95 instances of non-compliances of Accounting Standards.
Reports of Statutory Auditors on internal control of the companies indicated several weak
areas.

Arrears in accounts and winding up

Twenty five working PSUs had arrears of 64 accounts ranged from one to nine years as of
September 2013. The arrears need to be cleared in compliance with the provision of the
Companies Act, 1956. Out of the nine non-working PSUs, seven had gone into liquidation.
Arrears in respect of the remaining two non working PSUs ranged from three to five years.

(Chapter-I)


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31March 2013

A Review relating to Power Purchase Agreements entered into by MP Power
Management Company Limited/ Board/ Government of MP with Private Power
Producers for purchase of power and its operationalisation was conducted. Executive
summary of our audit findings is given below

Introduction

MP Power Management Company Limited (Company) under the administrative control of
Department of Energy, Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) is entrusted with the
responsibility of assessing the demand of power in the State and make arrangements for
meeting the demand by pooling all power purchases from different sources.

The Review conducted during May 2013 to July 2013 covers Power Purchase Agreements
(PPAs) entered into by GoMP Company with Independent Power Producers and
operationalisation of PPAs during the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13 including planning
and procurement of power.

Against the planned procurement of 1009 MW capacity of power through IPPs on long
term basis, the Company could procure only 207 MW during the period of review

The Company paid idle capacity charges to the extent of €66.77 crore during the period
from November 2011 to March 2013 for scheduling lower capacity from a gas based Power
Project.

Deviation from terms and conditions of Memorandum of Understanding and
Implementation Agreement in respect of PPA with BLA Power Private Limited resulted in
additional expenditure of € 1.53 crore due to purchase of two and half per cent of power at
regulated tariff rate instead of concessional tariff rate and committed additional cost of
73.83 crore.

The Company was deprived of availing cheap power from Sasan Power Ltd., for a period of
15 months due to postponement of commissioning date (COD) in all the six units of a
power project resulting in potential loss of % 1252.46 crore on account of purchase of
power from other sources.

The Company did not impose liquidated damages of € 10.89 crore as per PPA for tariff
based power for the delay in commissioning of the Bina Thermal Power Project beyond the
extension granted.

viii


Overview

Due to removal of capping of fixed charge rate, the Company had to incur an additional
expenditure of € 24.51 crore and committed to incur an additional cost of € 145.96 crore
towards fixed charges on procurement of power from Torrent.

There is no mechanism to verify landed cost of coal and its gross calorific value.
Due to deficiencies in the billing, payment procedure and deviation from norms, the
Company could not avail Rebate amounting to € 53.02 lakh from developers and also

incurred avoidable LPS of €6.97 crore.

(Chapter I)

2.2 Review on the working of Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog Nigam Limited,
Bhopal

A Review relating to Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog Nigam Limited was conducted.
Executive summary of our audit findings is given below

Introduction

Madhya Pradesh State Laghu Udyog Nigam Limited (Company) was incorporated in
December 1961 as a wholly owned Government Company to promote Small Scale
Industries (SSIs) by providing marketing, financial, technical and managerial assistance.
The Government of Madhya Pradesh has reserved 149 items, for preferential purchase
from SSI sector of the State, through the Company only, under its Store Purchase Rules
(SPR). The Company collects Service Charges/Commission from Suppliers on supplies
made by the suppliers to the Government Departments, which is a prime source of its
income.

Planning

Company had not prepared any promotional plan including financial plan for the
development of SSI units in the State. The Company did not assess the impact of the fairs
and Reverse Buyer Seller Meets. The actual achievement of business targets ranged
between 80.50 and 120.51 per cent during the period 2008-09 to 2012-13. The Company
could not achieve the targets fixed in MoU during the years 2009-10, 2011-12 and 2012-13.
The percentage of SSIs availing the marketing assistance ranged between 0.28 and 0.49
per cent of total SSI registered with Department of Industries, GoMP.

Marketing Activity

The business of Marketing department decreased from € 667.15 crore in 2008-09 to
& 529.60 crore in 2012-13 due to decrease in business of reserved items. The penalty
deducted for delay in Government supplies amounting to € 1.68 crore, till March 2012 has
not been paid to Government departments. The Company did not evolve a system to verify
the eligibility of SSI units with reference to Industrial Development Act for allowing proper
price preference of 10 per cent to state based units over all India tender.

1X


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31March 2013

The Performance of other departments of the Company

The divisions of Estate and Construction Department (E&C) split up the deposit works in
parts and kept the Probable Amount of Contract very proximate to © five lakh and avoided
publishing in news papers. The E&C Department adopted differential practice for
deduction and remittance of royalty to the government exchequer. The Company failed to
include an interest clause for delayed payments of bills in respect of construction of
Navodaya Vidyalaya buildings

The profitability of the emporia was mainly due to inclusion of commission earned on sales
of marketing items to Government Departments, agencies, bodies etc. for which marketing
wing of the Company was established. In Delhi and Rewa emporia, the stock holding was
more than their turnover. The Company has not framed any policy for disposal of non-
moving stock. The agreements entered into for renovation of five of its emporia did not
provide for levy of penalty in case of delay in completion.

The Raw Material Depot, Bhopal earned profit and Indore, Jabalpur and Gwalior Depot
earned profits due to inclusion of commission from marketing of products manufactured
by SSIs. There was shortfall in lifting of iron and steel items against the quantities to be
supplied by steel manufacturers, as the number of SSI units procuring iron and steel items
reduced from 146 in 2008-09 to 102 in 2012-13. The procurement of coal ranged from
72.45 to 86.26 per cent against the allotment and the Company could assist only 455 coal
consuming units as against total 19471 units in the State.

The turnover of the Company increased from हैं 151.92 crore in 2008-09 to
% 223.05 crore in 2011-12 (46.82 per cent), while the net profit of the Company increased
only from €13.18 crore in 2008-09 to €13.76 crore in 2011-12 (4.40 per cent). The profit is
mainly due to income earned through interest on Fixed Deposit Receipts, while its
operational activity was decreasing. The Receivables of the Company as at the end of
March 2008 was ¥ 5.72 crore, which increased to ¥ 9.44 crore as on March 2012. The
Company did not frame any long term investment policy to invest its surplus fund.

Manpower Management
The Company had not done reassessment of its manpower requirement.
Monitoring and Internal Control

There was no mechanism to ensure procurement of reserved items directly by the
government departments which were reserved for procurement through the Company. The
Company also does not have a system of verifying whether the beneficiary units were SSIs
in terms of Micro Small and Medium Enterprises Development Act, 2006. The Company
does not have an independent IT Cell/Wing to implement the information technology
system. The Company did not have a separate internal audit wing and the internal audit
work was assigned to the CA firms. The scope of internal audit is limited to physical
verification of stock and preliminary checking of accounts, leaving core area of activities
unaudited.

(Chapter II)



Overview

2.3 Review on Materials Management of Madhya Pradesh Power Generating
Company Limited.

A Review relating to Materials Management of Madhya Pradesh Power Generating
Company Limited was conducted. Executive summary of our audit findings is given
below

Introduction

The Madhya Pradesh Power Generating Company Limited, in order to operate and
maintain its three thermal plants (total capacity of 3687 MW) and seven hydel plants
located in the State, through its 21 stores is providing raw materials/inputs such as coal, oil
and critical inventory/spares. Hence, effective management of inventory ensures timely
availability of essential items for efficient operations with minimum blocking up of capital
by ascertaining needs of stores on time and arranging such materials in the most efficient,
economical and expeditious manner.

In order to review status of materials management in the Company a review on this
subject, covering transactions from 2010-11 to 2012-13 in respect of all stores attached to
three thermal plants was conducted in April to July 2013. The main findings, conclusions
and recommendations arising out of the review are summarised below.

Planning and scheduling for Procurement of material

In the absence of any norms fixed in terms of days for publication and awarding of tenders
from the date of receipt of indent from user department, the Company had taken time
ranging from 38 days to 246 days in publishing Notice Inviting Tender (NITs) and a total
of 238 days to 510 days for placing purchase orders, thus affecting its annual planned
requirement.

Procurement of materials

In 43 cases where the value of Purchase Order (PO) exceeded % five lakh, the requirement
was not published in newspapers as required under its established procedures. Further, the
Company had not only foregone an opportunity to obtain competitive rates, but also failed
to develop a secondary source of supply to overcome possible contingencies due to
placement of 66 POs on single tender basis.

Materials/spares valued € 9.75 crore, was procured without assessing the actual
requirement and were found to be lying in excess and inactive at various stores, resulting
in blocking up of Company’ s funds.

Coal losses written off in excess of limits permitted by Madhya Pradesh Electricity
Regulatory Commission (MPERC) amounted to ¥ 50.14 crore (2010-11), % 35.19 crore
(2011-12) and £40.50 crore (2012-13) respectively.

Due to excess holding of secondary oil beyond 60 days, the additional carring cost
amounted to% 1.40 crore to% 2.76 crore during 2010-13.

In 164 purchase orders valued € 102.84 crore, the Company waived the tender condition
for collecting10 per cent security deposit of € 6.64 crore from the suppliers. In respect of 52
purchase orders, material was not supplied even after lapse of two to three years.

x1


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31March 2013

Irregularities in inspection, receipt and issue of materials

As against Company’s clear instructions to issue Material Receipt Certificate (MRCs)
within seven days from the date of receipt of material at stores for processing payments to
suppliers, in respect of 325 supplies against POs, the management delayed issue of MRCs
for periods up to 209 days (Sarni), 918 days (Birsingpur) and 101 days (Chachai)
respectively.

(Chapter ID)

2.4 Review on Evaluation and Management of Build, Operate and Transfer
scheme of Madhya Pradesh Road Development Corporation Limited

A Review relating to Evaluation and Management of Build, Operate and Transfer

scheme of Madhya Pradesh Road Development Corporation Limited was conducted

Executive summary of our audit findings is given below

Introduction

Madhya Pradesh Road Development Corporation Limited, incorporated in July 2004 under
the Companies Act, 1956, is engaged in strengthening, upgradation and development of the
state highways and major district roads (MDR) in the State of Madhya Pradesh. The
Company is mandated to provide an efficient, adequate and proper road infrastructure in
the state.

During 2009-10 to 2012-13, the Company completed 16 project highways under different
models of Build Operate and Transfer (BOT) Scheme under Public Private Partnership
(PPP).

Overview of executed road projects

Out of the 16 completed projects, the Company completed 11 projects within the scheduled
period of completion. There were delays of 67 days to 564 days in achieving Commercial
Date of Operation in remaining five projects. The major reasons for delays were delay in
financial closure, delay in handing over of site and problems associated with Right Of Way

Erroneous Treatment of Environmental Mitigation cost

The Company allowed 25 per cent escalation on the environment mitigation cost also
treating it as construction cost. This resulted in higher payment to concessionaries
amounting to €62.71 lakh towards Viability Gap Funding.

Increase in project cost due to adoption of higher specification

Considering the present and projected traffic on the road, two lane standard on the
Khimlasa-Malthon section of the Bina-Khimlasa—Malthon project highway was not
warranted. Developing this section on two lane standard thus resulted in unwarranted and
avoidable expenditure of €14.37 crore towards proportionate VGF

xii


Overview

Payment of bonus and annuity under BOT (Toll plus Annuity) model

For the development of three project highways the Company formulated a new model of
PPP viz, BOT (Toll plus Annuity). Due to not reducing the construction period as
recommended by the Consultants payment of bonus for early completion resulted in
unintended benefit of €16.82 crore to the Concessionaires.

Selection of Public Sector undertaking as partner under PPP

Contrary to the principles of PPP the Company selected a public sector entity for the
development of the Bina—Kurwai-Sironj Project highway.

Incorrect fixation of toll

The Company did not formulate and document standard procedure for fixation of toll
rates. This resulted in unintended benefit to concessionaire to the tune of ₹ 37.43 lakh
towards higher toll rates.

Delayed remittance of premium by the concessionaire

The concessionaire of the Indore-Ujjain project highway did not remit to the Company the
premium amounting to €7.69 crore as on 30 September 2013.

(Chapter I)

A Review relating to working of Madhya Pradesh Warehousing & Logistics
Corporation was conducted. Executive summary of our audit findings is given below

Introduction

The Madhya Pradesh Warehousing and Logistics Corporation (Corporation) was formed
on 31 March 2003. The main functions of Corporation included creation of infrastructure
for storage of agricultural products, fertilizers, salt, gunny bales etc., undertaking
clearance of goods from the godowns and provide disinfestations services to farmers,
private parties etc. The Corporation is the nodal agency to provide warehousing facility to
the Madhya Pradesh State Civil Supplies Corporation Limited for storage of food grains
procured under Decentralised Procurement (DCP) scheme.

As on 31 March 2013, the Corporation has 2,887 godowns having total storage capacity of
54.73 lakh MT at 275 centres spread all over the State against the total storage capacity of
97.50 lakh MT available in the State. The review of the working of the Corporation
revealed following shortcomings



Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31March 2013

Planning

Memorandum of understandings (MoU) with the Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP)
for the years 2008-09 to 2012-13, intended to plan well in advance the activities to be
carried out in the forthcoming years, were signed at the end of the relevant financial years,
as such, the purpose of entering into MoUs was not achieved.

The requirement of land was not included in MoUs with GoMP sufficiently in advance. As
a result, the Corporation could not construct godowns at Chhindwada, Khandwa, and
Sehore due to non-availability of land.

The Corporation has not prepared any long term plan to increase its share in storage
capacity. The Corporation increased its storage capacity from 11.41 lakh MT in 2008-09 to
14.81 lakh MT in 2012-13 but its share in total storage capacity of the State had marginally
decreased from 15.83 per cent in 2008-09 to 15.18 per cent in 2012-13.

Construction of godowns

Against the target of storage capacity of 31,70,790 MT during the period 2008-09 to 2012-
13, the Corporation despite availability of funds, could construct total storage capacity of
6,53,350 MT.

Failure of the Corporation to execute agreements within the validity period of tenders
resulted in award of works in subsequent tenders at the higher cost by € 1.28 crore.

Due to delay in completion of works of convertible CAPs and pre- engineering godowns,
the Corporation could claim storage charges at the rate of % 24 only applicable for CAP
instead of €49 applicable for godown which resulted in loss of potential revenue of 8.86
crore.

Utilisation of godowns

In respect of own godowns and joint venture godowns its utilisation during 2008-09 to
2012-13 was less than the overall targets of utilisation. The Corporation stored foodgrain
in JV godowns under four branches despite availability of surplus storage capacity in own/
hired godowns, thereby incurred loss of revenue of 25.69 lakh.

Storage of foodgrain

Loss on account of non-achievement of specified storage gain and damaged stock was
worked out to € 91.50 crore as on 31 March 2013 but the Corporation did not analyse
reasons thereof and take remedial action for that.

The Corporation decided to use new kind of multi layered cross laminated film Covered At
Plinth (CAP) covers at large scale without any field trial. The Corporation incurred extra
expenditure of € 2.72 crore due to purchase of the new kind of CAP covers at higher cost
The Corporation incurred avoidable expenditure of € 44 lakh due to purchase of heavier
polythene covers for CAP compared to the standards set by Food Corporation of India.

खाए


Overview

Financial/budgetary management

Actual executed quantities in respect of 11 works exceeded the estimated quantities and
payment for such excess quantities valued at €4.57 crore were made to contractor without
approval of competent authority. In respect of 10 works, extra items of works values at
3.79 crore were executed without approval of the competent authority.

Human Resource Management

There was shortage of staff in all the groups in the Corporation against sanctioned
strength.

Monitoring and Internal Control Mechanism

Meetings of Executive Committee to review the functions of the Corporation, were not held
as per the prescribed frequency. Computer systems valued at ¥ 5.34 crore could not be
utilised by the Corporation since its installation in March 2008 up to September 2013 due
to not getting resolved the problems in software. No monitoring system was developed by
the Corporation to ensure timely renewal of lease deed for land.

(Chapter II)

Transaction audit observations included in the Report highlight deficiencies in the
management of Public Sector Undertakings involving serious financial implications.
The irregularities pointed out are broadly of the following nature:

Loss of € 33.44 crore in four cases due to non compliance with rules and procedure.
(Paragraphs 4.2, 4.6, 4.7 and 4.9)
Loss of € 1.55 crore was incurred in two cases due to deficient planning.
(Paragraphs 4.1 and 4.3)

Loss of % 7.18 crore in four cases due to non-safeguarding the financial interests of
organization

(Paragraphs 4.4, 4.5, 4.8 and 4.10)

Loss of % 168.12 crore was incurred in one case due to inadequate and deficient
monitoring.

(Paragraph 4.11)

Xv


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31March 2013

Some important observations are given below:

The Special Economic Zone (SEZ) Limited (Indore) did not avail Income Tax
exemption which resulted in avoidable payment of Income Tax and interest of
₹ 13.25 crore

(Paragraph 4.2)

Madhya Pradesh Madhya Kshetra Vidyut Vitran Company Limited suffered a
loss of revenue of € 84.84 lakh due to delay in issue of Supply availability notice and
billing for the enhanced demand

(Paragraph 4.6)

Madhya Pradesh Madhya Kshetra Vidyut Vitran Company Limited suffered a
loss of revenue of हैं 3.89 crore due to giving undue benefits to the consumer on
account of extending additional load before non fulfilment of sanctioned letter
conditions as per Electricity Supply code, 2004 and non levy of additional charges on
the excess load as per the MPERC Tariff

(Paragraph 4.7)

Madhya Pradesh Power Transmission Company Limited foregone revenue of
र₹ 23.13 crore through tariff and consequential interest loss of € 1.27 crore

(Paragraph 4.8)

Madhya Pradesh State Mining Corporation Limited failed to enforce the terms
and conditions of agreement for production of A grade rock phosphate resulting in
loss of revenue of % 14.71 crore and failed to levy penalty of हैं 73.55 lakh for
unexecuted quantity and the Company also extended undue benefit to the contractor
in awarding of contract for mining of Rock Phosphate resulted in avoidable extra
expenditure of & 4.82 crore

(Paragraphs 4.9 and 4.10)

The Madhya Pradesh State Mining Corporation Limited incurred a loss of

1.1 The State Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) consist of State Government
companies and Statutory corporations. The State PSUs are established to carry out
activities of a commercial nature while keeping in view the public welfare. The
State working PSUs registered a turnover of € 58237.27 crore as per their latest
audited accounts as of September 2013. The major activities of Madhya Pradesh
PSUs are concentrated in the power sector. The working PSUs of the State
incurred an overall loss of € 4031.63 crore in the aggregate as per their latest
audited accounts as of September 2013. They had employed 58459 employees as
of 31 March 2013.

1.4 Audit of Government companies is governed by Section 619 of the Companies
Act, 1956. According to Section 617. a Government company is one in which not
less than 51 per cent of the paid up capital is held by Government(s). A
Government company includes a subsidiary of a Government company. Further, a
company in which not less than 51 per cent of the paid up capital is held in any
combination by Government(s). Government companies and corporations
controlled by Government(s) is treated as if it were a Government company
(deemed Government company) as per Section 619-B of the Companies Act,
1956.

the provisions of the Companies Act, 1956.

1.23 The administrative departments have the responsibility to oversee the
activities of these entities and to ensure that the accounts are finalised and adopted
by these PSUs within the prescribed period. Though the administrative
departments concerned were informed of the arrears in finalisation of accounts on
a quarterly basis by Audit and the matter was taken up in September 2013 to the
Chief Secretary/ Principal Secretary Finance, no remedial measures were taken.

1.24 In view of the above state of arrears, it is recommended that the Government
should monitor and ensure timely finalisation of accounts in compliance with the
provisions of the Companies Act, 1956.

Introduction

MP Power Management Company Limited (Company) under the administrative control
of Department of Energy, Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) is entrusted with
the responsibility of assessing the demand of power in the State and make
arrangements for meeting the demand by pooling all power purchases from different
sources.

The Review conducted during May 2013 to July 2013 covers Power Purchase
Agreements (PPAs) entered into by GoMP Company with Independent Power
Producers and operationalisation of PPAs during the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13
including planning and procurement of power.

Against the planned procurement of 1009 MW capacity of power through IPPs on long
term basis, the Company could procure only 207 MW during the period of review

The Company paid idle capacity charges to the extent of € 66.77 crore during the
period from November 2011 to March 2013 for scheduling lower capacity from a gas
based Power Project.

Deviation from terms and conditions of Memorandum of Understanding and
Implementation Agreement in respect of PPA with BLA Power Private Limited resulted
in additional expenditure of € 1.53 crore due to purchase of two and half per cent of
power at regulated tariff rate instead of concessional tariff rate and committed
additional cost of €73.83 crore.

The Company was deprived of availing cheap power from Sasan Power Ltd., for a
period of 15 months due to postponement of commissioning date (COD) in all the six
units of a power project resulting in potential loss of € 1252.46 crore on account of
purchase of power from other sources.

The Company did not impose liquidated damages of 10.89 crore as per PPA for tariff
based power for the delay in commissioning of the Bina Thermal Power Project beyond
the extension granted.

Due to removal of capping of fixed charge rate, the Company had to incur an
additional expenditure of € 24.51 crore and committed to incur an additional cost of
145.96 crore towards fixed charges on procurement of power from Torrent.

There is no mechanism to verify landed cost of coal and its gross calorific value.


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31 March 2013

Due to deficiencies in the billing, payment procedure and deviation from norms, the
Company could not avail Rebate amounting to € 53.02 lakh from developers and also
incurred avoidable LPS of %6.97 crore.

2.1.1 The Government of India (Gol) notified a National Electricity Policy (NEP)
in February 2005 with the main objective to supply reliable and quality power in
an efficient manner and at reasonable rates. MP Power Management Company
Limited’ (Company) under the administrative control of Department of Energy,
Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) is entrusted with the responsibility of
assessing the demand of power in the State and make arrangements for meeting
the demand by pooling all power purchases from different sources.

The unrestricted maximum demand’ (UMD) for power in the State during 2007-
08 was 7200 MW and the Company/ State could meet the demand to the extent of
6436 MW resulting in a shortage of 764 MW, which was managed through load
shedding. In 2012-13, while the UMD increased to 9804 MW. the demand met
was 9412 MW leaving a shortage of 392 MW. Thus, the Company has been able
to largely meet the power requirements of the State as at the end of March 2013
Considering the improved power availability, GoMP in 2013, launched Atal Jyoti
Abhiyan with a view to provide 10 hours power supply to agricultural consumers
and uninterrupted supply of power to other consumers of the State.

In order to meet the gap between UMD and availability of power, the Company
prepares plans for procuring power from the different sources viz., State owned
generating stations, Central Generating stations and generation by Independent
Power Producers (IPPs) from which the demand would be met. The plan prepared
by the Company is submitted to GoMP for approval.

Power from IPPs is obtained through long term agreements and short term
arrangements. Long term agreements are entered into by the Company either
through the process of bidding or on the basis of Memorandum of Understanding
(MoUs) entered into by GoMP. MoUs are signed between the GoMP and the IPPs
proposing to set up power projects in the State and under the MoU, GoMP
undertakes to provide facilities such as providing land for the project from its own
sources or through acquisition, allotment of water for the project, assisting in
GoMP clearances for the project etc. In lieu of the facilities/ assistance from
GoMP, the IPP is required to provide five’ per cent of the power generated from
the project at concessional rate (Concessional power*) considering only the cost of
fuel. Besides, the MoU provides for first right of allocation of upto 30 per cent of

1 As a part of power sector reforms, Madhya Pradesh Power Trading Company Limited, Jabalpur,
was incorporated on 2 May 2006. The name of the Company was since changed to Madhya
Pradesh Power Management Company Limited (Company) with effect trom 10 April 2012.

> The actual point of peak demand is a single half hour or hourly period which represents the
highest point of customer consumption of electricity without any restriction.

57.5 per cent, if the fuel is obtained from captive coal block situated in Madhya Pradesh.

4 Concessional power represents the power agreed to be supplied by IPP on variable cost i.e., cost
of fuel.

14


Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

power of the Power Station’s installed capacity (tariff based power’) to the GoMP
or its nominated agency at rates as determined by the Madhya Pradesh Electricity
Regulatory Commission (MPERC). In pursuant to the terms of MoU, GoMP and
IPPs sign the Implementation Agreements (IA). On the basis of the MoUs/ IAs
signed between the GoMP and IPPs, the GoMP enters into Power Purchase
Agreements (PPAs) for concessional power and the Company enters into PPAs
for tariff based power. The Company being the nominated agency of the GoMP
receives the concessional power. Thus, through long term PPAs, the Company
obtains concessional power, tariff based power and bid based power’.

The details of long term agreements (PPAs) entered into by the Company for
procurement of power from conventional sources’ during the period 2006-07 to
2012-13 are given in Annexure-2.1.1. Besides, the Company has also entered
into 98 PPAs for procurement through non conventional sources®.

discussed with the Principal Secretary (Energy) and Managing Director of the
Company in an exit conference held in December 2013. Replies of the Company/
Government have been appropriately incorporated in the Report.

2.1.5 The objectives of the Review were to assess whether:

» The GoMP/ Company assessed the demand for power and planned for
creation of generation capacities under IPP mode in accordance with
National Electricity Plan/ Policy;

> The provisions of the Memorandum of Understandings (MoUs)/
Implementation Agreements (IAs)/ Model PPAs/ PPAs entered into by the
GoMP/ Company were in line with established guidelines/ rules/
regulations and not detrimental to the interest of the Company;

>» PPAs were operationalised as per its terms and conditions; and

>» An effective monitoring mechanism was in place.

2.1.6 The audit criteria for assessing the performance were:

> Electricity Act 2003 and Electricity (Supply) Act 1948;

> National Electricity Policies and Tariff Policies issued by Gol from time to
time;

> Guidelines, Terms and Conditions of Tariff issued by Central Electricity
Regulatory Commission (CERC)/ Madhya Pradesh Electricity Regulatory
Commission (MPERC) from time to time;

> Standard Bidding documents, Model PPAs framed by GoMP/ Gol, MoUs
and PPAs; and

> Decisions of the BoD and Management Information System reports.

2.1.7 The Audit findings are discussed below:

Target for procurement of power through long term agreements

2.1.8.1 As per NEP, the progress of implementation of capacity addition plans and
growth of demand would need to be constantly monitored and necessary
adjustments would be made from time to time.

Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

The details of capacity addition planned vis-a-vis actual achievement and shortfall
during the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13 are given in Annexure-2.1.2. It could
be seen from Annexure-2. 1.2 that against the planned additional capacity of 1009
MW on long term basis, the Company could add only 207 MW resulting in
shortfall of 802 MW in capacity addition to the end of March 2013. The shortfall
was mainly due to delay in completion of the projects planned. This resulted in
increased load shedding or purchase of power through short term arrangements.

We noticed that while the Company made plans to meet its entire demand without
any procurement from short term arrangements, the Company had to resort to
short term purchase of 2361.93 MUs at an average cost of € 4.86 per unit during
the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13.We observed that the average cost per unit of
power procured through long term basis during the above period was & 3.64 per
unit.

The Principal Secretary (Energy), during exit conference in December 2013,
stated that the responsibility of timely execution of the projects is entirely that of
the concemed IPPs and the GoMP or Company has a very limited role. He further
stated that the shortfall in long term procurement with reference to plan might
have resulted in resorting to load shedding etc. and the short term power purchases
were primarily to meet peak demand and therefore should not be linked to
shortfall in procurement on long term basis.

The fact however remained that the Company had to resort to short term
procurement at higher cost, though no such procurement was planned. Besides, if
supply had materialized through long term PPAs as planned, the Company would
have at least been able to reduce its short term purchases.

Delay in completion of Shri Maheshwar Hydel Power Project

2.1.8.2 PPA between the erstwhile Madhya Pradesh State Electricity Board
(Board) and Shri Maheshwar Hydel Power Corporation Ltd (SMHPCL) was
signed in November 1994, which was further amended in September 2005 due to
non-achievement of financial closure!!. As per the terms and conditions of PPA,
the scheduled Commercial Operation Date (COD) was March 2010 and extended
to October 2012 due to force majeure conditions. However, the COD has so far
(December 2013) not been achieved in the project.

The PPA, as amended, provides for termination of the contract in the event of
failure on the part of IPP in achieving COD as scheduled.

We observed that the project cost, which was estimated at % 736 crore in 1993
rose to हैं 3993 crore in 2011. The Company estimated in October 2011 that the
tariff’? would increase from हैं 1.51 per unit to 8.21 per unit due to increase in
project cost. We further observed that the expenditure incurred on the project till
March 2012 was % 2917.70 crore, representing 73 per cent of the estimated project
cost. Thus delay in the project might result in further increase in the overall

Contract for purchase of power from an expensive source

2.1.8.3 The Company entered (16 January 2007) into a Power Sale Agreement
(PSA) with PTC for purchase of 100 MW power, which had a back to back
procurement arrangement (August 2005) from the 1100 MW gas based power
project with Torrent Power Generation Limited (Torrent).

As per the provisions of the PPA between PTC and Torrent, Torrent was to ensure
fuel supply to meet the contracted capacity of 100 MW. Torrent had tied up for
4.2 Million Metric Standard Cubic Meter Per Day (MMSCMD) (for 77 per cent of
capacity) of gas as against total 5.45 MMSCMD of gas required for the project.
As Torrent could not enter into long term arrangement for supply of remaining
1.25 MMSCMD (23 per cent) of gas, it sourced the same from open market.
Torrent intimated (November 2011) PTC about the reduction in linkage of gas
from existing long term sources also and requested permission to procure the
remaining gas from spot market. As it would increase the weighted average
Energy Charges Rate (ECR) from हैं 2.70 to हैं 3.55 per Kwh, the Company decided
(November 2011) to procure only 77 per cent of contracted capacity from Torrent.
Thus, due to failure on the part of the Company to insert a clause for long term
fuel supply arrangement, the Company had to pay idle capacity charges" to the
extent of % 66.77 crore during the period from November 2011 to March 2013
(Annexure-2. 1.3)

Thereafter, the Company entered (April 2012) into another agreement with PTC to
purchase 35 MW power from the 382.5 MW gas based expansion project of
Torrent based on PPA (April 2012) between PTC and Torrent.

We observed that at the time of entering into subsequent agreement for purchase,
the Company was already aware of the problems of insufficient availability of gas
faced by the IPP and was already paying idle capacity charges. Therefore,
entering in to subsequent agreement for purchase of 35 MW power from the gas
based project when the IPP had not secured long term gas linkage lacked
justification.

The Government replied (October 2013) that there was a demand gap of around
2000 MW between off-peak and peak hours and as gas plants were suited to cope

3. Generation Capacity not utilised to full extent.

18


Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

with such instant variation, reserving capacity from such plants was desirable.
During exit conference, the Managing Director stated that supply of power from
1100 MW project were reasonable for a gas based power plant meant for
supplying peak power, till the signing of agreement for purchase of 35 MW from
the expansion project.

The reply is not acceptable as the Company was well aware about the worsening
situation of gas market at the time of entering into agreement in April 2012 and
the Company was paying idle capacity charges from November 2011 due to non-
availability of sufficient long term gas linkage. Reserving capacity from gas based
plants on the ground that it was meant for supplying peak power is not justified as
the Company had contracted for 24 hour supply for a period of 25 years and not
for supply during peak period.

2.1.9 The Gol has issued (January 2005) detailed guidelines for tanff based
bidding process for procurement of power competitively except in cases of
expansion of existing projects or State owned projects etc. As per clause 5.1 of
the Tariff Policy issued by the Gol in January 2006, all the future requirements of
power should be procured competitively by Distribution licensees

GoMP notified Madhya Pradesh (Investment in Power Generation Projects)
Policy 2010 (Power Policy) for procuring power from IPPs that are establishing
the power stations in the State through MoU route.

We noticed that out of 20 PPAs signed for total contracted capacity of 3365.5
MW of Thermal Power during 2006-07 to 2012-13 (Annexure-2. 1.1), the capacity
contracted through competitive bidding was only 4.46 per cent from one bidder
during 2010-11 at a levelised tariff'* of = 2.45 per unit. After 2010-11, the
Company did not initiate any action for procurement of thermal power through
competitive bidding. Instead, the GoMP/ Company signed 22 MoUs with IPPs
during the period subsequent to the date of notification of Power Policy till March
2013. Audit findings on the inadequacies in operationalisation of the PPAs are
discussed in succeeding paragraphs.

On scrutiny of records, we observed that Gol had allotted a coal block in June

entered into an agreement with BLA Industries Limited for supply of coal from

was to obtain fuel supply for the project from captive coal block, the GoMP/
Company would be eligible for concessional power of 7.5 per cent of the net
generated power from the project. Though at the time of signing of PPA for
concessional power, the fuel linkage from the coal block allotted as captive
dispensation to the IPP’s sister company was already in existence, GoMP did not
insist on supply of 7.5 per cent concessional power. This had resulted in foregoing

The Government stated (October 2013) that coal mines allotted to BLA Industries

generation of power by BLA.

The reply is not acceptable. MoC, 007 letter September 2012 clarified that coal
mine allotted to BLA Industries Limited was for Captive Dispensation. Further, in
exit conference, it was stated that the Company requested IPP to provide 7.5 per
cent of concessional power, which was not agreed to by the IPP. As BLA had
been consuming the coal extracted from the captive mine allotted to its sister
Company in the state of MP, the GoMP/ Company should have effectively
negotiated for 7.5 per cent of net generated power at variable cost as per
provisions of MoU.

2.1.9.2 The GoMP entered into the MoU with Reliance Power Limited, Mumbai
(RPL) in September 2007 for setting up 6X660 MW Thermal Power Project by
RPL which was to remain in force for a period of one year and the Government
may extend for such further period, as may be mutually agreed upon. As per
MoU, the GoMP/ Company was entitled to procure 5/ 7.5 per cent concessional
power and 30 per cent tariff based power for a period of 20 years. We observed
that the GoMP/ Company had not signed PPA with RPL so far (July 2013). We
further noticed that the validity period of MoU with RPL had lapsed in June 2010.
We also noticed that MoU did not contain any protective clause to ensure that it
does not lapse if there was delay in signing of PPA by the IPP. Absence of such
clause resulted in RPL getting the requisite assistance’® for the project from
Government without corresponding benefits to the State.

Procurement of Power through bidding process

2.1.9.3 MoP, Gol issued (January 2005) guidelines for procurement of power
through competitive bidding on long term basis as per the provisions of Section 63
of the Electricity Act 2003. The guidelines specify that procurement of power by
bidding can be through Case-1' and Case-2”” mechanisms

The Company under Case-1 for supply of capacity up to 2000 MW of power on
long term basis invited bids in June 2006. Reliance Power Limited, Mumbai
(RPL) after negotiation, agreed (April 2008) to supply 1241 MW at levelised tariff
of € 2.45 per unit from its Chitrangi Power Project in MP. The Company had also
entered into a MoU” with RPL for supply of 1188 MW of tariff based power in
September 2007 from the same project.

In October 2010, the Company requested RPL to sign the PPA for the 1241 MW
power offered through bidding process. RPL however informed the Company that
it would be willing to sign the PPA, only if the Company foregoes its right to
obtain 1188 MW of tariff based power under MoU obligation. The Company
informed (December 2010) RPL that the offer against bidding process (Case-1)
was independent and had no relation with the ‘tariff based’ power offered through
MoU route. RPL had however not signed PPA so far (July 2013). Delay in
signing the PPA and implementation of the Project would deprive the Company of
getting 1241 MW of power at low cost” for 25 years besides restricting/ derailing
the process of procurement through competitive bidding route.

We further observed that while RPL was not coming forward for signing PPA,
they were extending the validity of their bid and Bank Guarantee (BG) for हैं 37.50
crore towards bid bond from time to time. The Company has however not
forfeited the bid bond by encashing the bank guarantee.

The Government replied (October 2013) that RPL had not signed the PPA due to
non availability of full linkage coal and non achievement of financial closure of

procuring bio-mass and Wind Electric Generators (WEGs) based power through

In February 2012, the Company initiated bidding process for procurement of 200
MW solar power on long term basis. The tariff for solar power in terms of
MPERC tariff orders at the time of invitation of tenders was = 15.35/ 13.94 per
unit. After evaluation of bids, the Company entered into PPAs in October 2012
with Alfa Infraprop Limited and Welspun Solar Madhya Pradesh Private Limited,
for supply of 20 MW and 105 MW solar power respectively at their quoted tariff
of % 7.90 per unit and हैं 8.05 per unit respectively. The tariff obtained through
bidding was thus lower by 48.53 and 42.25 per cent of the MPERC determined
tariff.

We noticed that out of 98 PPAs entered into during 2007-08 to 2012-13, 96 PPAs
were entered through MoU route against the spirit of Tariff Policy 20067 and
guidelines for bidding issued by MNRE, Gol, and thus lost an opportunity to
obtain lower prices as seen in above two PPAs.

The Government stated (October 2013) that the tariff determined by MPERC for
Wind and Biomass projects was not very high as not much enthusiasm was shown
by generators on these projects. It further stated that availability of renewable
power to the extent of RPO had not yet been achieved and in the circumstances,
exploration of possibility to get power at rates less than MPERC tariff was not
feasible. Further during the exit conference it was stated in respect of non solar
based plants that the MNRE has not issued specific guidelines for purchase
through bidding.

Reply is not acceptable as the Company had so far not initiated the process of
bidding to determine the cost for bidding based power. The fact that the cost of
power through bidding in conventional and solar projects was less than prescribed
tariff decided by MPERC”’ indicate that the Company might have obtained lower
rates if it had opted for procurement through bidding. In bidding process, it would
be open to many generators resulting in lower rates as seen in the above two cases.
Further there was no restriction in attempting procurement of power through
bidding process for wind/ bio-mass plants.

Commercial Operation Dates

2.1.11.1 Commercial Operation Date (COD) is the date on which the power
supply would start from any unit of the power project. Timely completion of the
works is the essence of any agreement. Thus, fixation of COD in the PPA would
be a vital aspect as the power supply at a contracted capacity would start from this

date. We noticed instances where scheduled CODs were delayed due to delay in
providing the infrastructure and other facilities by GoOMP/ Company. The delay in
COD would result in either procurement of power at higher rates through short
term sources or load shedding. The cases of delay in scheduled COD and non-
levy of Liquidated Damages (LDs) are discussed below:

Delay in providing land and other clearances by GOMP

2.1.11.2 The Company entered into a PPA with Sasan Power Limited (Sasan) in
August 2007 for 1485 MW power out of the total capacity of 3960 MW of Sasan
UMPP. In terms of the PPA, the Company was required to hand over possession
of land for the power station and site clearance from Ministry of Environment and
Forest etc. The PPA also provided that the Power producer would have the option
to prepone COD. Sasan exercised the option to prepone the COD, which was
agreed to by the Company and accordingly a supplementary agreement was
entered into between the two parties in October 2008.

The details of scheduled CODs as per PPA, preponement of CODs at the request
of Sasan and the revised CODs due to default of the Company are given in the
Annexure-2.1.4. Sasan requested (July 2008) the Company to provide land and
forest clearances in terms of the supplementary agreement

Citing delay in meeting the obligations on handing over of land (9 months), forest
clearance (21lmonths), interconnection/ transmission facilities by GoMP/
Company as per the terms of the PPA etc., Sasan requested the Company to
postpone the CODs. The Company accepted (September 2010) the request of
Sasan and accordingly revised the CODs. As a result, the Company was deprived
of cheap power from Sasan for a period of 15 months resulting in potential loss of
& 1252.46 crore on account of purchase of power from other sources during the
corresponding period as detailed in the Annexure-2. 1.5.

The Government stated (November 2013) that there was no loss as the project was
completed as per the orginally scheduled COD and that the delay cannot be
attributed to delay in providing land as 95 per cent of the land required for the
project was already made available to the Sasan on time. Further during exit
conference Managing Director stated that Sasan attempted to aggressively bring
forward the commissioning date, which did not materialize as planned.

The reply is not acceptable as the Company accepted the plea of Sasan that the
COD was delayed due to Company's failure in meeting its obligations and
accordingly revised the COD. Further Unit I was delayed by four months from
original COD and Unit II, originally scheduled for COD in December 2013 had
not achieved COD till January 2014.

Delay in commissioning

2.1.11.3 The Company entered into a PPA (January 2011) with Bina for purchase
of concessional power (five per cent) and tariff based power (65 per cent) from

their 1250 MW Thermal Power Project to be established at Sirchopi, Tahsil
Bina, District Sagar, Madhya Pradesh. In terms of PPA for tariff based power,
Bina was liable to pay LD for delay in achieving COD as determined in the PPA.

The Bina sought (January 2012) four months extension for the COD of Unit-I
(Phase-I) due to unprecedented heavy rainfall. Company accepted to the request
and accordingly the COD was revised to July 2012. Bina achieved COD with a
delay of 30 days from the extended COD. No LD was however imposed for delay
beyond the period of extension granted by the Company.

Similarly, extension for COD of Unit-II (Phase I) sought by Bina was accepted by
the Company and COD was revised to February 2013. Bina however achieved
COD on 7 April 2013 with delay of 37 days. In this case also, no LD was
imposed by the Company for delay beyond the extension granted

Thus, delay in achieving COD by the Developer for Unit-I & II resulted in delay
in availing energy from the units. Besides, the Company failed to impose LD of
र₹ 10.89 crore for the delay as per provisions of PPA.

We further observed that the Company did not include the clause of LD in the
PPA for concessional power. As a result, the Company could not impose LD for
the delayed CODs.

The Government stated (November 2013) that an agreement was reached not to
levy LD as the Company had also defaulted in providing letter of credit (LC) on
time in terms of the PPA for which LD amounting to % 98.60 crore was payable
by the Company.

Reply is not correct as the penalty for not providing LC was payable only if there
was delay in commissioning test due to default of the Company. We did not find
any evidence that the commissioning test was delayed due to failure of the
Company to provide LC and thus the Company was not liable for any penalty.

Non-recovery of Liquidated Damages for short supply of power

2.1.11.4 As per PPA with Bina, the Developer was liable to pay LD to the
procurer at the rate of ¥ 10000 per day/ MW for delay of 60 days and हैं 15000 per
day/ MW for delay in making the required availability of contracted capacity.
However, the Developer was given option to supply energy from alternative

cost of procurement of power by the State.

Additional expenditure on Operation & Maintenance Expenses

2.1.12.1 PTC India Limited(PTC) entered (August 2005) into a PPA with Torrent
Power Generation Ltd. (Torrent) for purchase of power of 100 MW initially for
five years and to be reduced to 50 MW for ten years thereafter from their gas
based Thermal Power Project (1100 MW) established in Gujarat State. In January
2007, PTC entered into an agreement with the Company for supply of power of
same capacity. As per the agreement entered into by the Company with PTC, the
PPA between PTC and Torrent was an integral part of the contract.

Tariff for power from the project was to be determined by CERC on an
application/ petition filed by Torrent in terms of the PPA. Based on the petition
filed by the Torrent in June 2009, CERC approved the Capital Expenditure and
determined the tariff in January 2010. In its petition, Torrent claimed O&M
expenses, which were higher than the normative O&M expenses of % 14.80
lakh/MW to & 18.49 lakh/MW fixed by CERC for the period between 2009-10
and 2013-14. Higher claim was made by Torrent on the plea that advance class
machine/ technology was used in the power plant, which would increase the plant
availability from 85 to 88 per cent. The CERC, considering the plea, relaxed the
norms by invoking its power” under CERC (Terms and Conditions of Tariff)
Regulations 2009 and approved O&M expenses at rates between हैं 26.70 lakh/
MW and हैं 29.68 lakh/ MW, which was 60 to 69 per cent higher than the norms.

The Company accepted the additional burden of % 25.69 crore for the period from
April 2011 to June 2013 and committed additional expenditure of & 8.39 crore for
the remaining period of nine months (July 2013 to March 2014).

We observed that the higher plant availability would result in higher generation
and as a result higher revenues to the developer without any corresponding
benefits to the Company, which had to pay higher O&M expenses. The Company
however did not approach CERC for sharing of benefits accruing to the developer.
During the exit conference the Principal Secretary (Energy) accepted the facts and
stated that the Company would consider the feasibility of filing an appeal against
the CERC’s order.

2.1.13 The Annual Fixed charges were determined based on the financial
parameters outlined in MPERC/ CERC and the rate fixed in the PPAs should not
exceed in any case.

2.1.13.1 According to the PPA between Torrent and PTC, based on which
agreement for purchase of power between Company and PTC was drawn, the
fixed charges would not exceed as initially agreed between the parties. As per
Schedule-E annexed to the PPA between Torrent and PTC, the annual fixed
charges for the period commencing from the date of COD of the Project to
fifteenth anniversary of the COD would be हैं 0.83 per unit.

Torrent, however, requested PTC to increase the fixed charges due to increase in
the rate of interest on borrowing, working capital and increase in O&M expenses
etc. and PTC accordingly requested (June 2009) the Company either to increase
the tariff from हैं 0.83 to हैं 1.12 per unit or to accept the tariff as approved by
CERC. The Company sought (September 2009) opinion on the PTC’s request
from its legal advisor, who opined (November 2009) that the Company should
insist for enforcement of original PPA with capping of fixed at % 0.83 per unit
The Company without waiting for receipt of legal opinion, acceeded to the
demand of PTC (October 2009).

CERC determined (January 2010) the fixed charges at = 1.08 for the period from
March 2011 to March 2013 and the Company had started paying fixed charges
accordingly from March 2011.

Thus, due to failure of the Company to insist on terms agreed in the PPA/ PSA,
the Company had to incur an additional expenditure of  24.5lcrore (Annexure-
2.1.6) towards fixed charges for the period from March 2011 to March 2013 and
also committed to incur an additional cost of € 145.96 crore (Annexure-2. 1.6) for
the remaining period up to 151 anniversary of the COD as envisaged in the PPA.

During the exit conference the Principal Secretary (Energy) stated that the rates of
power shall be decided under Section 62 and 63 of the Electricity Act 2003 and
there was no provision for capping of fixed cost in the Electricity Act 2003
Reply is not acceptable as the Company’s own Legal advisor had opined that
Company should insist for enforcement of original PPA with capping of fixed
charges and no records were produced to Audit to show that capping of fixed
charges was against the provisions of the Electricity Act.

2.1.14.1 The Company entered four agreements with PTC for procurement of
power, based on the PPAs between PTC and IPP. Thus, the PPAs were integral to
the agreements with PTC. The details of the agreements entered are detailed in
Annexure-2. 1.7.

It could be seen from the Annexure-2.1.7 that the Company has to pay trading
margin to the PTC for power supplied from IPPs through the agreements. Till

attempted but failed to enter into PPAs due to its financial position. As IPPs
within the State did not have reservations in entering into PPAs with the
Company, the Company would have been able to enter into interstate PPAs also,
if it had intended to. This is further evidenced by the fact that the Company
entered into 117 PPAs (including one PPA entered for procuring power from
outside the State) with I[Ps without any intermediary and only four agreements
were entered into through PTC

Non-inclusion of clause relating to sharing of penalties levied by the Developers
on the fuel suppliers for short supply of fuel

2.1.15.1 Bina Power Supply Company Limited entered into Fuel Supply
Agreement (FSA) with Central Coalfields Limited (CCL) / South Eastern
Coalfields Limited (SECL) for procurement of coal for its 1250 MW thermal
power project. According to the FSA with CCL/ SECL were liable to pay
compensation to the Bina, if they deliver the coal below Annual Contracted
Quantity (ACQ). We observed that Bina received coal from CCL and SECL
below the ACQ due to which Bina had to purchase the deficit quantity of coal
from open market/ through imports. As a result. the rate of energy charges
increased from % 2.708 per unit to हैं 3.144 per unit during September 2012 to
January 2013. Due to higher rate of energy charges, the Company decided to
draw less power than contracted from February 2013 and paid idle capacity
charges to the extent of € 36.38 crore (Annexure-2. 1.9) based on 100 per cent of
the contracted capacity during the period from February to May 2013.

Further, we observed that while the Company allowed Bina to procure coal from
open market/ imported coal to meet shortfall in supply and also paid idle capacity
charges, the Company had not included an enabling clause for sharing of
compensation received by Bina from the fuel supplier as per the terms of FSA.

28


Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

The Government stated (November 2013) that the credit from fuel supplier was
received by the IPP for grade-slippage, oversized stones, short delivery etc. was
already being adjusted in landed price of primary fuel and therefore, non-inclusion
of this clause would not affect the energy charges annually. It was also stated that
there was a three year moratorium on compensation from CCL to Jaypee Bina.
The reply of the Government is not relevant as FSA did not cover short supply
with reference to agreement quantities. Further, according to FSA, for short
supply/ lifting, a separate claim has to be preferred for compensation and there
was no evidence from the record made available to audit that such compensation
was adjusted by Bina in the bills. Further, the moratorium on adjustment of
shortfall of supplies safeguards the interest of CCL and the IPP, but the additional
cost on open market purchase/import of coal due to shortfall in supply will have to
be suffered by the procurer. Therefore, the Company should take necessary steps
to insulate it from the burden arising from FSA by suitable changes to the PPA,
which was agreed to by the Managing Director.

Excess Land Allotment

2.1.15.2 As per PPA for procurement of grid connected solar power on long term
basis, land was to be allotted/ acquired at the rate of two hectares per MW for
Solar Photo Voltaic (PV) projects. We observed that in case of Acme Solar
Energy Private Limited, as against 50 hectares to be allotted, 74.914 hectares was
allotted, whereas in case of Welspun Solar M.P. Limited, as against 50 hectares to
be allotted 98.279 hectares was allotted by GoMP for their 25 MW each capacity
Solar PV Projects. Thus, land to the extent of 73.19 hectares was allotted in
excess of requirement.

Similarly, as per CEA Guidelines 2007, the land required for 6X660 MW Thermal
Power Station at Chitrangi with indigenous coal was 3280 acres (0.83 Acres/
MW), against which, 3466 acres were allotted by GoMP to RPL, resulting in
excess allotment of 186 acres.

The Government stated (November 2013) that the State Nodal Agency for new
and renewable energy viz., MP Urja Vikas Nigam provides necessary support to
facilitate land acquisition for solar projects and total land to be allotted for solar
developer depends on the terrain of the land as it would not be possible to install
solar panels on undulated land. In case of the thermal power project, Government
stated that land requirement was dependent on the location of the associated
auxiliaries like location of the reservoir, distance of coal mine from plant etc.
Reply confirms the fact that land was allotted in excess of requirement for the
Projects as per PPA and CEA guidelines. Allotment of land in excess of the
provisions results in higher cost of project and resultant higher tariff.

2.1.16 Energy bills would be raised by the IPPs on the Company on the seventh
day of each month basis for the concessional and tariff based power supplied
during the previous month. On receipt of power bills from the IPPs, the Company
scrutinises the bills, passes them and makes payment after pre-audit. On scrutiny

of the bills passed and payments made by the Company, the following

2.1.16.1 There was no mechanism for technical verification of landed cost of coal/
gas, Gross Calorific Value (GCV) of primary fuel and secondary fuel being used
and claimed as energy charges by the IPPs in their bills, either by the Company or

prevent the possibility of payment in excess of due

2.1.16.2 For PPAs entered into with the IPPs for procurement of tariff based
power, rebate clause for payment of any Bill before Due date was included,
whereas no such clause for rebate was included for concessional power. In two of
the eight PPAs* for concessional power, supply had commenced. We noticed
that the Company in certain cases, had paid bills in advance of the due date for
concessional power by one to 30 days.

The Government stated (November 2013) that the model PPA for concessional
power specifically excluded the rebate clause. During exit conference, the
Managing Director stated that the Government did not consider it appropriate to
provide for Rebate clause, since only payment made for concessional power was
for fuel, which was essentially reimbursement for the fuel used in the plant

Reply is not acceptable as the Company/ Discoms are paying interest on loans
taken for working capital needs and therefore payment of bills before due date
was not in the financial interest of the Company/ Discoms when there is no rebate
clause for early payment. Hence, the Company should either make payment only
on the due date or should consider inclusion of rebate clause in concessional
power PPA to reduce its financing cost.

2.1.16.3 In terms of a PPA entered into with Bina for procurement of 65 per cent
of the installed capacity of power, rebate was to be allowed if the payment was
made within 21 days from the date of receipt of bill as compared to 30 days
allowed in model PPA. Due to changes in PPA with reference to model PPA, the
Company had forgone a rebate of % 53.02 lakh on rebate during the period from
December 2012 to May 2013.

The Government stated (November 2013) that being a revival project, it was
agreed to provide payment within 21 days instead of 30 days. As the payment
period and rate of rebate considered for the purpose of tariff was not as per model
PPA, entering into PPA with terms beneficial to the IPP resulted in foregone

Payment of surcharge due to default in making timely payment

2.1.16.4 As per provisions of PPAs entered into with IPPs for procurement of non-
conventional source of power, when payment of any bills payable under the
Agreement was delayed by the Company beyond due date, Late Payment
Surcharge (LPS) would be payable by the Company to IPPs at prescribed rates.

Test check of energy bills submitted by the IPPs during the period from April
2012 to June 2013, indicated that the Company had defaulted in making timely
payment of energy bills in respect of 12 IPPs and incurred LPS amounting to =
6.02 crore (Annexure-2.1.10).

The Government stated (November 2013) that the receipts of the Company had
always been short of payment requirements and therefore delay in payments could
not have been avoided. It further stated that LPS has not been paid to any IPP
except Manganese Ore Industries Limited. The fact however remains that the
Company had paid bills in advance of the due date to IPP as commented in para
2.1.15.2 ibid and therefore the reply that the Company could not have avoided
delay in payments was not borne out of facts.

Payment of LPS due to deviation from norms

2.1.16.5 On scrutiny of PPAs, it was noticed that the Company had agreed for
payment period as 30 days instead of 60 days as prescribed in MPERC (Terms
and Conditions for Determination of Generation Tariff) Regulations 2009/ CERC
(Terms and Conditions of Tariff) Regulations 2009. As a result, the Company had
incurred an avoidable LPS of हैं 94.97 lakh in respect of four PPAs, from which
power supply had started. Besides, by adopting payment period at variance with
regulations, the Company would incur additional LPS in all the PPAs during the
currency of the contracts

The Government during exit conference replied that the matter would be brought
before the MPERC for factoring the reduction of due days for making payment of
bills from 60 days to 30 days while determining the final tariff.

2.1.16.6 Internal Audit is an essential tool to strengthen the management by
constructive appraisal of the operations including the review of internal control,
policies, plans and procedures

We observed that the Company did not have an internal audit wing and the work
of internal audit was carried through Chartered Accountant firms. Scope of
Internal Audit work did not include the work relating to core activities of the
Company viz., billing and payments made to power suppliers, purchase of power,
sale of power, letter of credit or escrow agreements entered by the Company etc,
and was limited to assistance in preparation of accounting statements, statutory

31


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31 March 2013

compliances and other issues in connection with the Company’s financial
statements.

Due to narrow scope of internal audit, the work of audit committee of the
Company regarding review of intemal audit report and action taken thereon was
also very limited.

The Statutory Auditor in his report on the Annual Accounts for the year 2011-12
opined that the scope of internal audit was insufficient and not commensurate with
the size and nature of the business of the Company and needs further
strengthening.

Thus, there is a need for strengthening of internal audit to address the risk areas in
the changing business environment.

During exit conference, the Principal Secretary (Energy) agreed to strengthen the
monitoring and control of the Company.

The Company failed to achieve the targeted procurement of power on long term
basis. The Company deviated from MoU/ IA/ Model PPAs/ PPAs entered into
with IPPs and failed to enter into agreement with RPL even after finalisation of
bids.

The Company failed to insist on terms agreed in the PPA/ PSA resulting in
additional expenditure towards fixed charges on procurement of power due to
removal of capping of fixed charges. It incurred avoidable expenditure on
commission due to procuring power through intermediary instead of procuring
directly. The Company failed to include the clause relating to sharing of penalties
levied by the Developers on the fuel suppliers for short supply of fuel.

The Company did not levy liquidated damages for delay in commissioning
beyond the period for which extension was granted. Monitoring mechanism for
processing of bills and payment procedure were deficient resulting in loss of
rebate and avoidable payment of LPS. Scope of work for Internal Audit is not
commensurate with the size and operations of the Company.

The Company needs to

>» adhere to its planning in procurement of power and ensure compliance of
the Guidelines/ Policy issued by Central/ State Governments from time to
time;

> implement and adhere to the provisions of the Acts/ model PPAs/ PPAs;

>» explore an alternative system to avoid purchasing power to meet long term
power needs through power trading company;

> insert a suitable clause in the PPA to insulate it from the loss arising due to
the failure of the Fuel Supplier/ IPPs in delivering/lifting the contracted
quantity of fuel.; and

» strengthen its monitoring mechanism and internal audit.

2.2 Review on the working of Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog Nigam Limited,
Bhopal

Executive Summary

Introduction

Madhya Pradesh State Laghu Udyog Nigam Limited (Company) was incorporated in
December 1961 as a wholly owned Government Company to promote Small Scale
Industries (SSIs) by providing marketing, financial, technical and managerial
assistance. The Government of Madhya Pradesh has reserved 149 items, for
preferential purchase from SSI sector of the State, through the Company only, under
its Store Purchase Rules (SPR). The Company collects Service Charges/Commission
from Suppliers on supplies made by the suppliers to the Government Departments,
which is a prime source of its income.

Planning

Company had not prepared any promotional plan including financial plan for the
development of SSI units in the State. The Company did not assess the impact of the
fairs and Reverse Buyer Seller Meets. The actual achievement of business targets
ranged between 80.50 and 120.51 per cent during the period 2008-09 to 2012-13. The
Company could not achieve the targets fixed in MoU during the years 2009-10, 2011-12
and 2012-13. The percentage of SSIs availing the marketing assistance ranged between
0.28 and 0.49 per cent of total SSI registered with Department of Industries, GOMP.

Marketing Activity

The business of Marketing department decreased from 667.15 crore in 2008-09 to
& 529.60 crore in 2012-13 due to decrease in business of reserved items. The penalty
deducted for delay in Government supplies amounting to £1.68 crore, till March 2012
has not been paid to Government departments. The Company did not evolve a system to
verify the eligibility of SSI units with reference to Industrial Development Act for
allowing proper price preference of 10 per cent to state based units over all India
tender.

The Performance of other departments of the Company

The divisions of Estate and Construction Department (E&C) split up the deposit works
in parts and kept the Probable Amount of Contract very proximate to five lakh and
avoided publishing in news papers. The E&C Department adopted differential practice
for deduction and remittance of royalty to the government exchequer. The Company
failed to include an interest clause for delayed payments of bills in respect of
construction of Navodaya Vidyalaya buildings

The profitability of the emporia was mainly due to inclusion of commission earned on
sales of marketing items to Government Departments, agencies, bodies etc. for which
marketing wing of the Company was established. In Delhi and Rewa emporia, the stock
holding was more than their turnover. The Company has not framed any policy for
disposal of non- moving stock. The agreements entered into for renovation of five of its
emporia did not provide for levy of penalty in case of delayin completion.

The Raw Material Depot, Bhopal earned profit and Indore, Jabalpur and Gwalior
Depot earned profits due to inclusion of commission from marketing of products

34


Chapter- II- Review relating to Government Companies

manufactured by SSIs. There was shortfall in lifting of iron and steel items against the
quantities to be supplied by steel manufacturers, as the number of SSI units procuring
iron and steel items reduced from 146 in 2008-09 to 102 in 2012-13. The procurement
of coal ranged from 72.45 to 86.26 per cent against the allotment and the Company
could assist only 455 coal consuming units as against total 19471 units in the State.

The turnover of the Company increased from © 151.92 crore in 2008-09 to
₹ 223.05 crore in 2011-12 (46.82 per cent), while the net profit of the Company
increased only from € 13.18 crore in 2008-09 to € 13.76 crore in 2011-12 (4.40 per
cent). The profit is mainly due to income earned through interest on Fixed Deposit
Receipts, while its operational activity was decreasing. The Receivables of the Company
as at the end of March 2008 was € 5.72 crore, which increased to % 9.44 crore as on
March 2012. The Company did not frame any long term investment policy to invest its
surplus fund.

Manpower Management
The Company had not done reassessment of its manpower requirement.
Monitoring and Internal Control

There was no mechanism to ensure procurement of reserved items directly by the
government departments which were reserved for procurement through the Company.
The Company also does not have a system of verifying whether the beneficiary units
were SSIs in terms of Micro Small and Medium Enterprises Development Act, 2006.
The Company does not have an independent IT Cell/Wing to implement the
information technology system. The Company did not have a separate internal audit
wing and the internal audit work was assigned to the CA firms. The scope of internal
audit is limited to physical verification of stock and preliminary checking of accounts,
leaving core area of activities unaudited.

[ Introduction

2.2.1 The Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog Nigam Limited (Company) was
incorporated (December 1961) as a wholly owned Company of the Government of
Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) with a view to promote Small Scale Industries (SSIs) by
providing marketing, financial, technical and managerial assistance. Activities of
the Company include finalising rate contract for reserved items under Stores
Purchase Rules (SPR) as well as non reserved items', civil construction works and
renovation works of Government Organisations, selling handloom and handicraft
items, arranging procurement of raw material’ required by the SSIs. extending
testing facilities* for the products, etc. Thus, the working of Company is multi-
dimensional. The Company has three Regional Marketing Offices’, four Raw

‘ Ttems which are not covered under SPR

material depots’, eight Emporia’, eight Estate and Construction Divisions’ and
two Testing Laboratories*®

To ensure the growth of Company, the GoMP fixes targets every year for various
activities of the Company and signs a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU)
with the Company. The GoMP has reserved 149 items, for preferential purchase
from SSI sector of the State, through the Company only, under its SPR. The
Company is recognised as a nodal agency not only for procurement of reserved
items but also for the non reserved items not covered under SPR. In order to meet
its expenses, the Company collects service charges/commission from Suppliers on
the supplies made by the suppliers to Government Departments/ State Public
Sector Undertakings etc. which is a prime source of income to the Company.
Further, the Company ears supervision charges on the construction works
executed on deposit work basis. The Company operates emporia for the sale of
handloom and handicraft items

The Management of the Company is vested in a Board of Directors (BoD)
consisting of six Directors” (March 2013). The Managing Director (MD) is the
Chief Executive Officer and is assisted by five Chief General Managers’
followed by Deputy Chief General Managers and General Managers as per
organizational chart at Annexure-2. 2.1.

The Paid-up capital of the Company was % 2.83 crore as on 31 March 2013. The

The audit methodology adopted with reference to the audit criteria consisted of

2.2.3 We explained the audit objectives to the Company during the Entry
Conference held on 18 April 2013, which was attended by the Managing Director
of the Company and representatives of Government. Audit findings emerging
from the Review were reported (August 2013) to the Company and the State
Government and discussed (November 2013) in the Exit Conference, attended by
the Additional Chief Secretary (ACS), Department of Commerce and Industries,
GoMP and Managing Director of the Company. The views expressed by the
Management/Government in the Exit Conference and through their replies
