Industrial Promotion Policies

2.2.6 Department of Commerce, Industry & Employment (C&I), Government of
Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) framed Industrial Promotion Policy in 2004, which was
subsequently revised in 2010 with the main objective of promoting industrial
investment in the State with special attention on promotion of small and medium
enterprises. As determined by the above Industrial Promotion policies, the
Company was required to take steps on the following:

» to establish vendor development and linkage between SSI and other units
in developed industrial growth centres such as Pithampur, Malanpur,
Mandideep to achieve zero inventory level for improving competitiveness
and work efficiency of SSI & tiny sector units;

> to extend uninterrupted and easily available financial support to small
industries through banks and financial institutions;

> expansion of marketing activities by organisation of buyers-sellers meet,
trade fairs etc. and establishment of Urban hats (Mela) at Indore and other
suitable places for expansion of marketing and display of small scale
industries products under the marketing scheme of Development
Commissioner (Handicrafts), Government of India.

> In the interest of small scale industries, complete transparency would be
brought in working procedure of the Company and getting store purchase
rules amended to increase supplies through SSI

Improper management of indents

2.2.9.1 Store Purchase Rules-14” provides for placing of indents by the indenting
Departments on the Company for supply of reserved items. The State
Government directed (October 2007) all the indenting departments to send the
indents electronically to the Company to make the process more transparent and
to avoid receipt of indents from departments by the Company through
unauthorised intermediaries”*. In compliance to the above Government order, the
Company intimated all the indenting departments, in February 2009, that indents
be sent through electronic means.

We observed that:

» The company continued to receive indents by post and through
messengers, which amounted to violation of Government instructions. The
Company had not received any indents through electronic means till
March 2013. Thus, the Government’s objective of keeping intermediaries/
mediators away and bringing transparency to the process had not been
ensured by the Company. Besides, this also resulted in extra expenditure
to the departments as discussed in succeeding paragraph.

The Government accepted (October 2013) the fact and stated that the Company
has started (April 2013) obtaining indents through electronic medium.

» In 53 cases of the supply of cement during the period 2011-12 and 2012-
13 and the supply of RCC fencing poles during the year 2011-12, indents
obtained from the department were retained by mediators/suppliers for
periods ranging from 14 days to 310 days in case of Cement and for
periods ranging from 50 days to 83 days in case of supply of RCC fencing
poles. After the rates for the items were revised upwards by the
Company, the indents were submitted to the Company for issue of supply
orders. This resulted in extra cost of हैं 23.20 lakh on cement and हैं 3.67
lakh on procurement of RCC fencing poles to the departments and
corresponding undue benefit to the suppliers

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that the respective
departments and not the Company was responsible for the belated receipt
of indents. Further, Company stated that it brought the issue to the notice

Allowance of price preference to ineligible units

2.2.9.2 The Company invited” tenders for procuring galvanised mild steel tubes;
un plasticised poly vinyl chloride rigid pipes/casing pipes etc. to meet the
requirement of various Government Departments on all India basis. As per the
prevailing policy of the GoMP, in respect of all India tender, price preference was
to be given to state based SSI units up to 10 per cent over the lowest offer
received against the tender.

The Industries (Development and Regulation) Act, 1951 (IDA) read with
notifications/clarifications/orders issued from time to time, defines SSI and
ancillary industrial undertaking.”

The Company placed”* supply orders on M/s. STL Exports Ltd by giving price
preference for SSI unit of the State. Besides, orders were also placed on M/s. STL
Extrusion एच. Ltd and M/s. Siddhartha Marketing Services Ltd. All the three
companies were part of the Siddharth Group of Industries as some of the Directors
of these companies were having business interest in other group companies by
way of directorship/equity holding.

We noticed that the companies of this group do not fall under the definition of SSI
in terms of the IDA and therefore ineligible for price preference, as the gross
block of assets and investment in plant and machinery of Siddharth Group was
more than % 5 crore as per their Balance Sheet for the year 2007-08 to 2011-12.
Price preference given to M/s STL Exports Ltd in procurement of GI pipe to the
extent of 11.63 crore was therefore not in order. This not only resulted in undue
benefit to the supplier but also resulted in extra cost in the form of price
preference to the indenting departments of the Government.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that these companies were SSI
units and have no relation with each other and not being managed and controlled
by Directors of other related companies.

The reply is not acceptable as the group companies have significant influence in
each other as per records available. Besides, we noticed that the Company did not
verify the investment in Plant & Machineries from the audited Balance Sheet of

* December 2010 and August 2012

?5Investment in plant & Machinery more than twenty five lakh Rupees but does not exceed tive
crore Rupees determined as SSI. Further, where an industrial undertaking is a subsidiary of, or is
owned or controlled by. any other industrial undertaking or undertakings and if the total
investment in fixed assets in plant and machinery of the first mentioned industrial undertaking and
the other industrial undertaking or undertakings clubbed together exceeds the limit of investment
prescribed.

the above mentioned three companies to determine their eligibility for price

Irregularities noticed in finalisation of tenders by marketing department

2.2.9.3 Tender for supply of laptop, computers and desktop with peripherals was
invited in March 2008, wherein eligibility for participation was limited to firms
having tumover of not less than € 500 crore. One SSI unit viz. HLBS Pvt. Ltd.
Bhopal, which had a turnover of less than ¥ 500 crore offered its rate against the
tender, though the firm did not fulfill the eligibility criterion. The Company
however considered the offer as it was from a SSI unit and placed (2008-09)
orders on the firm for supply of computers valued at हैं 18.90 lakh. Changing the
eligibility criteria at the time of placement of order not only vitiates the tender
process but also result in depriving other SSI units an opportunity to participate in
the tender. Besides, inviting bids from non-SSI units, when such items are being
manufactured by SSI was against its own objective of promotion and development
of SSIs in the State.

The Government stated (October 2013) that the offer of HLBS Tech. Pvt. Ltd. was
considered as it was a SSI unit in the State and after this tender, the Company has
incorporated the relaxed criteria of past performance and qualification for SSI
units of the state in the tender document. The fact remained that the Company
deviated from its objective of promoting SSIs in the State by incorporating a
condition of turnover. Further, finalising the procurement without giving
opportunity of participation to other SSI units led to undue benefit to the firm.

Supply of DI pipes from the firms having doubtful business integrity

2.2.9.4 The Company invited (March 2012) all India tender for supply of duct iron
pipes to meet the requirement of Government Departments.

We noticed that during the process of evaluation of the tender, the Company
became aware that one of the tenderers M/s Rashmi Metalics Limited, Kolkata
had been black listed by Government/ Government bodies in some states due
evasion of revenue, involvement in fraud and irregularity in supplies. The
Company, however, placed orders of % 1.06 crore on the firm during 2012-13.This
not only resulted in undue benefit to the firm but also risked the interest of the
indenting departments in obtaining quality supply on time.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that there was no provision in
special terms and conditions of tender documents and general terms and
conditions of the tender to debar the firms that were involved in evasion of
Government revenue and the firm therefore could not be debarred by the
Company. It was further stated that the point was noted for future compliance.

The reply is not acceptable as the Company was aware that the material supplied
by the firm to various Government undertaking/Departments in the country were
sub-standard and of poor quality. The Company should therefore have re-invited

would lead to compromising quality, which is against the interest of GoMP.

Performance of Estate & Construction (E&C) Department

2.2.10 The Company has an Estate and Construction wing (E&C). As per the
articles of Association of the Company, civil works relating to construction,
maintenance and administration of industrial estates and industrial areas for small
scale industries are only permitted to be undertaken.

The Company however, under took construction of schools, hostels, Primary
Health Centers of Government on deposit work basis. The wing also undertook
interior decoration and renovation work of offices of other Corporations/offices
and gets supervision charges as remuneration. The Company received funds of
174.79 crore and utilised & 163.00 crore during 2008-09 to 2011-127”,

Execution of deposit works

2.2.10.1 As per the requirement of the depositing agency, the Company prepares
estimates (including contingencies and supervision charges) and accordingly,
receives funds for the execution of deposit works after getting Administrative
Approval from the concerned agency. The work is generally executed as per
provisions of the Public Works Department (PWD) Manual of the State/Schedule
of Rates (SoR) and other related orders and instructions of the PWD department/
Company from time to time. The works awarded by the Company to contractors
were also based on SoR of PWD.

We observed that the Company had so far (December 2013) not adopted some of
the provisions of manual and orders of the PWD department as discussed below:
> The PWD introduced (April 2010) the system of e-tendering for the
tenders where estimated cost was = 10.00 lakh or above. In May 2012, e-
tendering was introduced for all works irrespective of value. The
Company however introduced (September 2011) e-tendering only for the
works where estimated cost was in excess of & 25 lakh.
>» The GoMP also started (February 2007) a portal for e-procurement in
construction works, supply of materials etc. by the goverment
departments, agencies, boards, public enterprises and other organizations
of the State, where tenders could be uploaded for better transparency and
wider publicity. The Company however uploaded only the tenders where
estimated cost was above & 25 lakh.
>» As per the PWD Manual, tenders were to be accepted by the competent
authority as per actual cost” of the work and not as per the Probable
Amount of Contract (PAC) of the work. We however noticed in 35 test
checked cases, where the actual cost of the work ranged between हैं 25.47
and 34.77 lakh, that the tenders were accepted by the CGM (E&C)

Lack of transparency in tender publication in News Papers

2.2.10.2 As per the decision (March 2010) of the Managing Director, in line with
the provisions of PWD manual, all the works relating to construction activities
whose estimated cost of works exceeded हैं five lakh were to be published in news
papers for wider publicity.

During test check of tenders invited by the divisions, we observed that in 790
cases, the divisions recorded the PAC below @ five lakh (ranged between? 4.50
lakh and हैं 4.99 five lakh) and avoided publishing in news papers. In 13 test
checked cases, we noticed that the actual expenditure ranged between हैं 7.23 lakh
and हैं 24.96 lakh.

We further observed that the deposits received from various Government
agencies/departments against works were in lump sum and estimates were also
prepared for the entire work to be executed for the same client and at same
premises”.The works were however split up for finalisation of tenders in parts
and thereby the requirement of sanction from higher authorities and publication in
news papers were avoided. Three test checked cases, where the works were split
into 34 parts are given in Annexure-2. 2.3.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) accepted the fact and stated that the
Company had issued (August 2013) orders to follow e-tendering for all the works,
irrespective of the amount involved and publication of tenders in news paper.
Managing Director agreed in exit conference (November 2013) with the views of
the audit and assured to enquire the details of works which were split up to avoid
approval by higher authorities.

Inventory management in Emporia

2.2.11.1 The term inventory management describes the effective method of
controlling objects and activities and ensuring that they get to the right place at the
right time all within a cost parameter. The emporium-in-charge procures materials
based on the market trend, price, previous rate, quality, customer demand of goods
to be sold etc. The inventory held by different emporia during the period 2008-09
to 2012-13 has been tabulated in Annexure-2. 2.4.

We noticed that:

» The Company was not having norms prescribed for holding of stock in
various emporia either through purchase manual or through executive
instructions.

The percentage of closing stock to sales was maintained by Bhopal
emporia was the lowest among the emporia which ranged between 11.33
and 18.22 per cent during the year 2008-09 to 2012-13.

The closing stock in Rewa (2011-12 and 2012-13) and New Delhi (2008-
09 and 2009-10) was more than their annual turnover leading to unwanted
accumulation of stock and resulted in blockage of funds apart from
deterioration of stock which was alarming.

Vv

Vv

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that the Company considers its
inventory as a whole and examines the inventory on the basis of year wise
inventory level

As the emporium wise inventory level during the period covered under the review
was alarming, the Company needs to fix norms for inventory holding for
individual emporium.

Goods sent on approval not returned

2.2.11.3 The term ‘Goods sent on approval’ means arrangement under which
items of durable nature are provided to a prospective customer for a pre purchase
trial. These items are returnable in resalable condition within seven days from the
date of issue. if not accepted for purchase. If the goods are not retumed within
seven days, the goods are liable to be billed.

We observed that non durable goods worth हैं 24.41 lakh were sent on approval to
customers but were not returned or adjusted through sales in the stock statement
as on March 2013.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that the goods sent on approval
had been reduced to हैं 5.12 lakh (October 2013) by either billing the customers or
by obtaining return of goods. While action for liquidating the balances of the
goods sent on approval had been taken by the Company, after being pointed out
by the Audit, no details as to how much was sold or taken back and physical
condition thereof was furnished. Further, the Company had not introduced a
monitoring mechanism for regular adjustment of goods sent on approval.

Assistance to emporia weavers/craftsmen

2.2.11.4 One of the objectives of the Company was development of weavers and
artisans through sale of their products in emporia by marketing support. However,

we observed that growth in the number of venders to whom marketing support

particularly Indore, Jabalpur and Gwalior emporia. It was also observed that there

taken purely on commercial basis and the purchase cannot be driven by objectives
of their development only, though the number of weavers and artisans can be
increased subject to availability and the quality of products offered.

The objective of the emporia was to promote the weavers and artisans of the State,
but the Company procured material mainly from manufacturers and suppliers
instead of weavers and artisans. Thus, the objective of providing marketing
assistance to weavers and artisans had been very limited.

Renovation of emporia

2.2.11.5 With a view to cater to the demands of changing shopping habits of the
consumers and increasing the turnover, Company decided to renovate its emporia
to make it more consumer friendly with the core objective of promoting the
products of the weavers and artisans of the state. The Company accordingly, took
up work of renovating अंडर of its emporia during the period from 2008-09 to
2012-13 at a cost of € 3.55 crore. While sales and profit after renovation increased
in two’ emporia, it decreased in Jabalpur emporium. In respect of remaining
three emporia, renovation work was completed only during 2013-14.

The Company enters into agreement with the contractors entrusted with the work
of renovation specifying the time period for completion. As the company is in the
business of trading handicraft and handloom products through its emporia, any
delay in completion of renovation work will have direct impact on its sales and
profit arising from such sales.

We noticed that in the agreements entered into for renovation of six of its
emporia, no clause for levy of penalty in case of delay in completion of work was
incorporated in five’ contracts. In the case of renovation of Indore Emporium,
the agreement with M/s Antique Interior Designer, Gwalior contained a penalty
clause according to which, a penalty of हैं 10000 for each day of delay was
leviable. The renovation work of Indore Emporium was completed by the
contractor after a delay of six and half months for which penalty amounting to =
19.70 lakh was leviable. The Company however levied a penalty of only हैं 4.20
lakh. This resulted in short levy of penalty of € 15.50 lakh and undue advantage
to the contractor to that extent. Besides, due to delay in renovation work,
Company lost consignment sale of € 57.00 lakh in Indore emporium.

ऊँ Emporia- Bhopal, Kolkata, Indore. Jabalpur, Gwalior and Rewa

In the absence of an enabling clause for levy of penalty, in respect of Bhopal and

work was completed after delay of 10 and 12 months respectively

The Government in its reply (October 2013) accepted the fact and stated that the
tender condition related to renovation of emporium have been revised and suitable
penalty clause would be included in future renovations. Further, it stated that the
committee considered the delay of 42 days for penalty in respect of Indore
emporium and imposed penalty of % 4.20 lakh.

The reply is not acceptable as penalty leviable as per the agreement was & 19.70
lakh against which the company levied only हैं 4.20 lakh. Further, the agreement
did not stipulate any clause under which either time extension can be granted or
delay in execution could be condoned/reduced. We recommend that the Company
may consider uniform clauses in the agreements entered into by all the emporia
after ensuring that financial interests of the Company are secured.

Cancellation of supply orders placed on SSI units

2.2.12.1 The Company executes annual rate contract with SSI units for supply of
stainless steel utensils, which is an item reserved under Store Purchase Rules
(SPR). Indents for procurement of utensils are mainly received from Woman and
Children Welfare Department (WCWD), GoMP. We noticed that Company
placed supply orders of % 47.10 crore for the supply of stainless steel utensils, of
which orders amounting to हैं 19.87 crore were cancelled by WCWD during the
period covered under Review, on the ground that the quality of utensils supplied
was poor. The Company did not take up with WCWD against cancellation as the
defective quantity could have been replaced under the terms of agreement with the
SSI units, besides imposition of penalty on them.

Further, this was a reserved item under SPR and it is obligatory on the
departments to procure the same from the Company only. The Company however
did not watch if the requirements for cancelled items were met by WCWD
through procurement from the market subsequently. As SSI units would have
already mobilized resources to supply material to WCWD as per orders placed,
cancellation of order without compensation to SSI units would affect the growth
of the SSI units in the State. We however noticed that no provision existed in the
agreement between WCWD and the Company for imposing penalty on
cancellation of order.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that WCWD, at its own
discretion cancelled the order and Company was left with no option but to cancel
the supply orders given to the SSI units. Further, it was also stated that the matter
of compensation to SSI units would be taken up with the concerned department.

Performance of Business Development Cell (BDC)

2.2.13 As per the coal supply policy, the Company was nominated by the GoMP
for supply of coal to small and medium enterprises in the State. The Business
Development Cell (BDC) of the Company carries out the activities to assist SSI
units in procurement of coal. The Company enters into a Coal Supply Agreement
with the South Eastern Coalfields Limited (SECL)/Western Coalfields Limited
(WCL) for procurement of coal to be allotted to SSI units of the State. The
Company earns five per cent as commission on the basic price of coal. The
position of allocation by coal companies and distribution of coal to SSI units is
given in the table 2.2.5

_ Table 2.2.5
a ca

It could be seen from the above table that the lifting of coal against allocation
ranged from 72.45 to 86.26 per cent. The Company assisted only 455 coal
consuming units as against total 19471 units“’in the State, which represented only
2.33 per cent of units having coal as main source of power. The reasons for short
lifting were mainly due to inferior grade of coal.

We further observed that, as per the coal allotment procedure, the utilization of
coal for every quarter by the units has to be verified by the General Manager
(GM) of the District Trade and Industries Center (DTIC) and the Company before
allotment of next quarter coal. The Company, however, did not have a system of
verification and is relying on the certificate given by the unit and countersigned by
the GM (DTIC).

The Government in its reply (October 2013) accepted the fact and the CGM
(BDC), in the exit conference stated that due to inferior grade of coal, the lifting
was at lower side and also assured to take up the matter with coal companies.

The Company should devise a mechanism to make it possible for the small scale
units to draw coal under this arrangement so that the entire quantity allotted is
lifted, which in turn would assist the development/growth of SSI units.

Failure to monitor realisation from Sundry Debtors

2.2.16 The Company did not have any age wise and party wise records of Sundry
Debtors. The sundry debtors of the Company stood at % 5.72 crore, € 7.28 crore, =

Fund Management

2.2.17 The Company had been investing its internally generated surplus funds and
unspent deposits received from various Government departments for execution of
civil works in Fixed Deposits (FDs). The investment in FDs increased from ईैं
116.27 crore (March 2009) to हैं 209.18 crore (March 2013) and the Company
eamed interest of ¥ 46.35 crore during 2008-09 to 2011-12. The Company did not
frame any long term investment policy to invest its surplus fund and a mechanism
to assess the requirement of fund for ongoing deposit works to derive maximum
returm through prudential investment plan.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) assured to frame a long term policy
for investment of surplus funds.

Non remittance of penalties recovered from SSIs

2.2.18 As per clause 23(1) of the agreements executed between the Company and
SSIs, in case supplies are delayed or not found according to the
specification/substandard supplies or for any other reason, a penalty equivalent to
two per cent per month subject to maximum of 10 per cent of the value of
supplies is to be imposed and recovered from SSIs. We noticed from the records
of the Company that penalty deducted against supplies to Government
departments was हैं 1.68 crore as on 31 March 2012, which included हैं 1.45 crore
pertaining to the period prior to 2008-09.

We further noticed that the Company retained above amounts without any specific
consent or approval of the State Government and had shown the same as liability
not remitted (as on March 2012) into the accounts of the State exchequer. This
resulted in unauthorised retention and use of penalties by the Company as its
working capital.

Charging of excess commission

2.2.19 The Company introduced (March 2001) Mrignayani Vipnan Seva (MVS)
scheme for the supply of various items"! to the Goverment Departments through
its Emporia and also issued® instructions for its implementation. The main
objectives of the scheme were to fulfill small requirements of the Government
Departments at regional level. Initially, 17 items were identified to be covered
under the scheme for marketing and supply purpose. During the period 2009-13,
five emporia at Bhopal, Indore, Gwalior, Jabalpur and Rewa secured business of
& 122.37 crore and earned services charges/commission of % 5.90 crore.

As per the Rule 16 of the SPR, the service charges/commission charged by the
Company should not exceed two per cent. The emporium department of the
Company charged service charges/commission in MVS scheme at the rate five per
cent on unreserved items and two per cent on reserved"? items. As a result, excess
commission of € 3.45 crore was recovered from the Government departments.

Thus, charging of excess three per cent commission was against the rule 16.

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that charging of five per cent
service charges was decided by the BoDs of the Company and MVS tenders were
now floated through e-tendering system. However, MD in exit conference
(November 2013) agreed to review the rates of commission and take corrective
action.

It is true that five per cent service charges were approved by the BoDs but,
relevant provision of Rule-16 of SPR was not mentioned in the Agenda items.

was fixed at 535 in 1986 and same has not been reassessed so far (March 2013).

As the business of the Company is decreasing as discussed in Para No.2.2.7, the

Lack of monitoring by management

2.2.21 The units/field offices prepare periodical performance reports manually and
submit to Head Office of the Company. We. however, noticed that the monitoring
mechanism in the Company suffered from the following deficiencies, which had
impact on its objective of assistance in development and growth of SSI:

» There was no mechanism to assess the quantum of direct purchases made

by Government departments of items that were reserved for procurement
through the Company in terms of the SPR. As a result, the Company was
unable to take up this issue with the Government for ensuring that the
business reserved for the Company is not diverted by the
department/agencies of the Government.
The Company did not get the SPR revised for the last more than 20 years
though many of the items reserved for procurement through the Company
have became obsolete and not being indented by the departments. Some
of the new items dealt by the Company are unreserved items and it would
help the SSI sector, if such items are got reserved by revision in the SPR.

Implementation of Information Technology System

2.2.22 The Company does not have an independent IT Cell/Wing to implement
the information technology system in the Company, despite having tumover of =
223.05 crore in 2011-12 under different segments of business. Further, 25 units
located in and outside of the State have not been connected through a centralised
server and management has to depend on different software installed at different
departments to gather information. The Marketing department of the Company
through e-portal published tenders, monitors supplies’ The system however does
not exhibit on e-portal, the value of supply orders placed on different units as a
measure of complete transparency despite its requirement in industrial policy of
GoMP. Therefore, the system in use needs up gradation to suit the working of the
Company and should be compatible with entire working to save time, cost and
access by all officers/genuine users.

Internal Control

2.2.23 Intemal control system is an essential part of the Management activity. An
efficient and effective system helps the Management to achieve its laid down
objectives, procedure and financial proprietary discipline. We observed the
following deficiencies in the intemal control system-
» There was no internal control for recovery and adjustments of very old
sundry debtors. (Para number 2.2.16)

» Systems for timely disposal of non-moving, damaged and surplus
inventory accumulated in the emporiums of the Company were not in
place. (Para number 2.2. 11.2)

>» The Company does not have a system of verifying a unit as SSI with
reference to MSMED Act, 2006 with the related audited balance sheet of
the unit from time to time. As a result, the Company is not in a position to
ensure that the benefits meant for SSI are not availed by the units which
had become medium enterprises. (Para number 2.2.9.2)

>» As per the coal allotment procedure, the utilization of coal for every
quarter by the units has to be verified by the GM (DTIC) and the Company
before allotment of next quarter coal. The Company however did not have
a system of verification and is relying on the certificate given by the unit
and countersigned by the GM (DTIC). The Company does not also check
with the DIC as to whether the countersignatures were made on
verification. (Para number 2.2.13)

» The tenders floated by marketing, emporia, RMD and E&C departments
were not routed through pre audit at corporate level and concurrence of
finance department H.O. was not obtained before finalisation thereof. The
system needs to be introduced for avoiding any leakage of revenue, extra
cost or deviation from compliance of statutory provisions/manual

» There was no structured mechanism for analysing the reasons for unit wise

variance between actuals and budget which would make it difficult for the

Company to take effective financial measures for overcoming

inadequacies and fund management.

Absence of a purchase manual relating to emporia business and

accounting/ audit manual of the company.

v

Internal Audit

2.2.24 To keep proper watch on various activities performed by various wings, an
adequate internal audit system which is commensurate with the size and nature of
business of the Company is essential. It was noticed that the Company had a
separate Internal Audit Wing headed by General Manager (Internal Audit) and a
Manager, which undertakes physical verification of stock. The Company also use
the services of CA firms for audit of cash book, Bank book with related vouchers,
Bank reconciliation, advances to employees etc. Thus, the internal audit in the
Company is limited to physical verification of stock and preliminary checking of
accounts leaving scrutiny of core area of contracts, tenders etc. unaudited.

The Company had also not framed any Internal Audit/Accounts Manual and the
internal audit reports had not been submitted to the BoDs. Thus, internal audit
system was deficient and ineffective.

58


Chapter- II- Review relating to Government Companies

The Government in its reply (October 2013) stated that due to shortage of staff,
intemal audit wing could not be continued and internal audit work has been
assigned to Chartered Accountant firms. The Statutory Auditors in their Audit
Report for the year 2011-12 also expressed that the internal audit system needs to
be further strengthened.

The reply was not acceptable as the work assigned to intemal auditors was
restricted up to preliminary checking of transactions leaving scrutiny of contracts
and tenders unaudited.

The Company had not prepared any promotional and financial plan for the
development of SSI units in the State and did not assess the impact of the fairs and
RBSMs. The Company could not achieve the targets of business fixed in MOUs
during the three years up to 2012-13. The percentage of SSIs availing the
marketing assistance was insignificant.

The business of marketing department of the Company decreased due to decrease
in business of reserved items, non-adherence to the GoMP orders regarding
management of indents. The company did not evolve a proper system to verify the
eligibility of SSI units for allowing price preference.

Civil deposit works were split up in parts to avoid publishing tenders in news
papers and the Company also failed to introduce e-tendering system fully to make
the entire process of finalization of tenders and issue of work order transparent
and free from defects. The E&C divisions adopted differential practice for
deduction and remittance of royalty to the government exchequer and non-
inclusion of interest clause in the agreement.

The profitability of the emporia was mainly not due to counter sales. There was no
policy for disposal of non- moving stock, goods sent on approval. Company did
not levy penalty as per clause of agreement against renovation work of emporia.

The Company could not lift the total quantity of iron and steel allocated by steel
manufacturers. Similarly, it could not also lift the total allocated quantity of coal
and thus could not provide assistance to SSIs as planned. The testing fee structure
was not revised since 2011-12 in respect of Technical labs.

The profit of the Company was mainly due to income eamed through interest on
FDRs, while its operational profit was in decreasing trend. The receivables
management was poor and lacked timely reconciliation. The company has not
done reassessment of manpower.

The Company does not have an independent IT Cell/Wing to implement the
information technology system and the functioning of intemal audit wing was
deficient and ineffective.

Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

Introduction

The Madhya Pradesh Power Generating Company Limited, in order to operate and
maintain its three thermal plants (total capacity of 3687 MW) and seven hydel plants
located in the State, through its 21 stores is providing raw materials/inputs such as
coal, oil and critical inventory/spares. Hence, effective management of inventory
ensures timely availability of essential items for efficient operations with minimum
blocking up of capital by ascertaining needs of stores on time and arranging such
materials in the most efficient, economical and expeditious manner.

In order to review status of materials management in the Company a review on this
subject, covering transactions from 2010-11 to 2012-13 in respect of all stores attached
to three thermal plants was conducted in April to July 2013. The main findings,
conclusions and recommendations arising out of the review are summarised below.

Planning and scheduling for Procurement of material

In the absence of any norms fixed in terms of days for publication and awarding of
tenders from the date of receipt of indent from user department, the Company had
taken time ranging from 38 days to 246 days in publishing Notice Inviting Tender
(NITs) and a total of 238 days to 510 days for placing purchase orders, thus affecting
its annual planned requirement.

Procurement of materials

In 43 cases where the value of Purchase Order (PO) exceeded & five lakh, the
requirement was not published in newspapers as required under its established
procedures. Further, the Company had not only foregone an opportunity to obtain
competitive rates, but also failed to develop a secondary source of supply to overcome
possible contingencies due to placement of 66 POs on single tender basis.

Materials/spares valued € 9.75 crore, was procured without assessing the actual
requirement and were found to be lying in excess and inactive at various stores,
resulting in blocking up of Company’ s funds.

Coal losses written off in excess of limits permitted by Madhya Pradesh Electricity
Regulatory Commission (MPERC) amounted to €50.14 crore (2010-11), €35.19 crore
(2011-12) and €40.50 crore (2012-13) respectively.

Due to excess holding of secondary oil beyond 60 days, the additional carrying cost
amounted to & 1.40 crore to% 2.76 crore during 2010-13.



Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31 March 2013

In 164 purchase orders valued % 102.84 crore, the Company waived the tender
condition for collecting10 per cent security deposit of हैं. 6.64 crore from the suppliers.
In respect of 52 purchase orders, material was not supplied even after lapse of two to
three years.

Irregularities in inspection, receipt and issue of materials

As against Company’s clear instructions to issue Material Receipt Certificate (MRCs)
within seven days from the date of receipt of material at stores for processing payments
to suppliers, in respect of 325 supplies against POs, the management delayed issue of
MRCs for periods up to 209 days (Sarni), 918 days (Birsingpur) and 101 days
(Chachai) respectively.

2.3.1 Madhya Pradesh Power Generating Company Limited (Company) is a
wholly owned Company of Government of Madhya Pradesh (Government) and is
engaged in generation of electricity. It is the successor entity of erstwhile Madhya
Pradesh State Electricity Board (MPSEB) and came into being w.e.f. 1 June 2005

Management of the Company is vested in a Board of Directors (BODs) appointed
by the State Goverment. The day to day operations are carried out by the
Managing Director who is the Chief Executive of the Company and is assisted by
the Executive Directors, Chief Engineers, Superintending Engineers an
Executive Engineers.

To operate and maintain its three thermal plants and seven hydel plants (total
capacity of 3687 MW) located in the State, the Company is maintaining 21 stores
(thermal- 11 and hydel-10) to provide coal, oil and other critical inventory/spares.
Besides, open land/stock yard/tanks are also being used for storing coal and oil

While on one hand it is essential to ensure availability of raw materials viz. coal
and oil, stores and spares for uninterrupted operations and maintenance o:
generating power stations, on other hand excess inventory holding leads to
additional carrying cost on account of interest, storage and handling charges,
insurance, record keeping, inspection and risk of deterioration in quality. which
adversely affects the profitability of the organization. Hence, management o:
inventory ensures timely availability of essential items with minimum blocking up
of capital in the most efficient, economical and expeditious manner.

2.3.2 The objectives of audit were to examine whether:

> system of planning and scheduling for procurement of material existed and
was adequate and required funds were provided for procurement;

> inspection, receipt and issue of materials were according to prescribed
procedures and

62


Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

» system for identifying and timely disposal of obsolete, slow and non-
moving materials was adequate.

2.3.3 Audit criteria, for the purpose of audit were derived from following sources:

> orders and guidelines of Goverment of Madhya Pradesh Electricity
Regulatory Commission (MPERC). MPSEB Purchase and Stores manual/
procedures of Company, terms and conditions of tender/supply etc:

> norms laid down by the Management for holding raw material and stock of
spares and categorisation of slow moving. non-moving and obsolete items;

> Agenda and Minutes of BoDs meetings

> delegation of financial powers at various levels for procurement.

2.3.4 We reviewed records and systems/procedures relating to procurement and
storing of coal, oil and stores/spares for three years 2010-11 to 2012-13 of 11
stores’ of three thermal plants.

The scope and methodology of Audit were explained to the Management in an
entry conference held in April 2013. The audit was conducted during April to July
2013. Audit findings arising out of audit were discussed in an exit conference held
with the Government and Management on 21 October 2013. The views and
replies of the Government/Management on audit findings have been appropriately
incorporated in the review.

2.3.5 We acknowledge the co-operation extended by Government and the
Company in providing necessary information, records and clarifications for
smooth conduct of the review.

2.3.7 Timeframe for planning of procurement of material

2.3.7.1 Up to 2012-13, the Company has not evolved any policy to have an
Advance Procurement Plan in place. However, the user departments on the basis
of Anticipated Annual Requirement (AAR) of the items mandated for centralised
procurement and also the other spares and stores that are required in a year, send
indents to the Material Management (MM) department at Corporate Office who is

Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

responsible for centralised procurement” of goods. The requirement of users is
compiled and consolidated by MM department before publication of Notice
Inviting Tender (NIT). Thereafter, the tendering procedures are followed and with
the prior approval of competent authority, orders are placed on the successful
suppliers/ bidders.

During test check of AAR of centralised items, which were consolidated and
finalised by Chief Engineer/Material Management (CE/MM), Jabalpur, we
noticed that no time limit for sending the annual requirements was prescribed by
the MM department. Time limit or norms were also not fixed for finalising
indents. In the absence of time limit, the indents of spares required in a year are
received from user departments after the commencement of the year for which
indents are made, thereby defeating the purpose of preparation of annual indents.
We further noticed that in 15 out of 141 cases of centralized purchases test
checked, the time taken by the MM department to publish the NIT ranged from 38
to 246 days from the date of receipt of indents and 238 to 510 days for award of
purchase orders (POs) (Amnexure-2.3.1 & 2.3.2).Similarly, for procurement of
stores and spares other than centralised items like grinding balls, other than mill
bearing spares etc., the MM department took 30 to 160 days from the date of
receipt of indents for publishing NITs and 210 to 441 days for award of POs
(Annexure-2.3.1 & 2.3.2).

We further observed that the delivery schedules in respect of 8 out of 47 POs test
checked and placed by MM Department spilled over to the subsequent year.
Abnormal time taken for placement of order and spilling of delivery schedules to
subsequent year would defeat the objective of procurement based on Anticipated
Annual Requirement planning and the need/requirement of generating stations are
not met affecting efficiency of plants, which would result in further wear and tear.

The Management stated (July/October2013) that inviting tenders after ensuring
actual requirement at site, evaluation and vetting of indents and re-confirmation of
requirements from user departments normally required six to eight months. It was
also stated that compilation and consolidation of requirements was a time
consuming process and there could be further delays on account of clarifications
obtained from bidders. The fact remains that Para 2.5 of the Memorandum on
Purchase Procedures clearly stipulates that the time required for various factors
having a bearing on supply should be centralised and indents raised accordingly
to ensure that supply materialised as per requirement.

stores should be fixed. As the levels were not fixed in terms of the manual in all
the stores, the Additional Secretary of the Company directed (August, 2009) all

2.3.8 Placement of purchase order on single tender basis

2.3.8.1 As per the established purchase procedures, the Company had to call for
tenders/quotations from at least three approved vendors/parties and wherever, the
value of materials indented exceeds हैं 5.00 lakh, tenders were to be published in at
least two daily newspapers.

We observed that while the MM department publishes NIT for centralised
procurement, there was no practice of publishing tenders exceeding हैं 5.00 lakh by
the generating plants. We also observed that quotations were being called from a
single supplier by the generating plants. During test check of 235 purchase orders*
relating to three thermal plants, we noticed that 66 tenders were called from a
single party only, of which in 43 cases‘, though estimated value of supply
exceeded हैं 5.00 lakh, tenders were not published in news papers

It was replied (October 2013) by the Management that critical items of
special/proprietary nature where reverse engineering could not be done are being
procured from OEMs on single quotation basis. However, audit observed that
even the suppliers of commonly used spares/consumables were being treated as
OEMs and single tender was invited. Further, the Company failed to explore
alternate sources of supplies from open markets to increase its vendor base by
publishing tenders and was dependent on single source. This not only resulted in
violation of established procedures and resultant foregone opportunity to obtain
competitive rates but also in failure to develop a secondary source of supply to
overcome possible contingencies.

Unwarranted procurement of materials/spares resulted in non-moving of
inventories and blocking of funds

the items procured indicated that the items that were to be procured through
centralised purchase were directly procured on the plea of urgent need/critical for
operations. However, we observed that many of these items were not utilised by
the concerned plants for periods ranging from one to twelve years resulting in
blocking of Company's funds. Details in respect of few purchase orders, where

Coal losses on account of transit, stacking and handling exceeding MPERC
norms

2.3.8.3 Coal is one of the major inputs and important raw material required for
generation of power and consumes the major portion of Company’s working
capital. Adequate planning and proper arrangement for timely supplies is required
to ensure uninterrupted operation of the generating plants.

The Company, to meet its coal requirements, procures coal from the mines of
South Eastern Coalfields Limited and Western Coalfields Limited located at a
distance of 70 to 450 Km from the three thermal power stations. During
transportation of coal and its handling at the plant, some quantity is lost by wind,
moisture, spontaneous combustion and as carpet coal®. Besides, loss also occurs
due to inaccuracies found in weighment process. Taking all these factors into
consideration, the Madhya Pradesh Electricity Regulatory Commission (MPERC)
has fixed a normative allowable loss of 0.2 per cent for pit head and 0.8 per cent
for the non-pit head generating stations. As any loss exceeding permissible limits
will have to be absorbed by the Company, it is necessary for the management to
take concrete steps to arrest/reduce these losses

The table 2.3.2 indicates the total coal procured and losses incurred on transit,
Stacking and Handling (S&H) of coal during last four years ending 31 March
2013

commissioning of plants or replaced during capital overhauls and are capitalised to assets, whereas
consumption of O&M spares are charged to P&L account

° Carpet coal : Formation of soft carpet, comprising of coal and dust which result in loss of coal

In order to study the causes for such losses, the Company constituted
(March/April 2008) a committee of Engineers who recommended creating
sufficient infrastructure by providing concrete flooring of coal yards, fencing,
training personnel in stacking and handling etc. Similar measures to control coal
losses were also recommended (November 2010) by the Director (Vigilance).
Audit observed that even after a lapse of three years except for work undertaken to
construct a compound wall. no other recommendations were implemented by the
Company.

We also observed that even though MPERC has not fixed separate norms for
carpet coal losses as it is a part and parcel of stacking and handling process, for
which norms already existed. The Company however conceptualized and provided
for the carpet coal losses accumulated for over 47 years at 14 coal yards in its
three thermal plants in the books of accounts for the year 2009-10 and wrote off
(March/July 2012) 114217 MTs valued रैं 17.82 crore As carpet coal losses was not
separately allowable in terms of MPERC regulations, the Company had absorbed
this additional loss on account of carpet coal from its own accruals.

The Management replied (October 2013) that MPERC has gradually reduced the
norms for coal losses. It was also stated that boundary walls were being
constructed and security arrangements enhanced, to further reduce losses. The fact
remains that non adherence to norms fixed by MPERC would result in increased
losses to the Company and steps to minimize coal losses as per recommendations

Excess inventory held in respect of secondary oil

2.3.8.4 After coal, the second most consumable inventory in power plants both in
physical and financial terms is secondary oil which includes furnace oil (FO),
High Speed Diesel (HSD) and Light Diesel Oil (LDO), which is used alternatively
to ignite/fire to maintain the required temperature/heat in the boilers. Secondary
oil is being procured from centrally owned PSUs on rate contract basis. The
annual consumption of secondary oil was about 59933 KL, 57204 KL and 49924
KL during the years 2010-11, 2011-12 and 2012-13, respectively.

MPERC allows holding of inventory of 60 days of consumption of oil for the
purpose of determining the cost of working capital. Thus, the Company, by
efficient management in procurement and distribution of oil to the generating
plants, could improve its profitability.

A review of monthly consumption of secondary oil vis- a- vis stock of closing
balances of inventory held in terms of number of days indicated that the holding
of secondary oil was in excess of the norms prescribed by MPERC for calculating
the interest on working capital. The details of inventory held and the impact in
terms of carrying cost in the generating stations is given in Annexure 2.3.4.

It can be observed from the Annexure 2.3.4 that all the three thermal plants were
holding an average excess stock of secondary oil (worked out at 85 per cent Plant
Availability Factor)’ ranging from 434 KL to 2351 KL (FO) and 555 KL to 1016
KL (LDO) considering the requirement for 60 days. The excess holding was to the
extent of 16 to 336 days requirement (i.e., even after holding inventory upto 60
days) respectively. Based on their value, the additional carrying cost on holding
stock in excess of sixty days requirement amounted to हैं 1.40 crore, हैं 1.75 crore
and & 2.76 crore during the years 2010-11, 2011-12 and 2012-13 respectively

While accepting the audit observation, the Management replied (October 2013)
that necessary action to bring in improvement would be undertaken.

single bid in September 2010 from Voith Turbo Private Limited, Hyderabad
(VTPL). Accordingly, a purchase order valued at हैं 23.57 lakh was placed (April
2011) with VIPL. Supply was received on 30 August 2012.

In this connection, we observed that the spares stated to be required during AOH*®

whenever AOH are planned by the Company, it should ensure availability of

delayed jeopardising operations. As these items are plant specific, their alternate
use was not possible till next overhaul after three to five years. Hence, financial
planning for receipt of materials and its utilisation should have been
ensured/priomitised.

Non-compliance of guidelines/instructions

2.3.8.6 Government of M.P. issued directions (August/September 2010) to
introduce E-Tendering system in the Company from October 2010. The Company
has not introduced E-Tendering for orders placed till June 2013, which not only
amounted to non-compliance of instructions but also deprived the Company of the
benefits of competitive bidding and strengthening of vendor base.

We observed that with the approval of Government, the Company started the e-
tendering process from July 2013 by uploading tenders in the GoMP website

The Management stated (October 2013) that procurement through e-tendering
system has commenced from 1 July 2013. The fact remains that the Company
started the e-tendering system only after a delay of over 30 months after issue of
instructions by GoMP in September 2010, while the Goverment departments had
started e-tendering from October 2010 itself.

Irregular exemption and waiver of Security Deposit and Earnest Money Deposit

2.3.8.7 As per Clause 15 of standard tender document and circular instructions
issued from time to time, the Company has to collect 10 per cent of cost of
purchase order value as Security Deposit (SD) against performance Guarantee
(PG) from the suppliers. The Company also has the system of collecting one time
fixed amount ranging from हैं 3 lakh and हैं 5 lakh towards permanent of security
deposit (PSD) from the suppliers and exempt them from paying SD for each order

8 The material/spares required during overhaul cannot be put to use till next overhaul due after

the Company

Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

placed with them. Besides, Public Sector Undertakings like Bharat Heavy
Electricals Limited, Indian Oil Corporation Limited, Hindustan Petroleum
Corporation Limited, etc. are exempted from payment of SD. The tender notices
issued for procurement included the condition for payment of security deposit
amounting to 10 per cent of the value of order.

During test check, we observed that in 164 purchase orders (Centralised-1 No and
Local- 163 Nos) valued € 102.84 crore placed on private suppliers between April
2010 and March 2013, the Company did not collect 10 per cent of SD amounting
to % 6.64 crore (includes 5 per cent differential SD of % 3.64 crore in one case on
= 72.80 crore) as contemplated in the tender documents. In 52 of these purchase
orders, value ranging between & 0.14 lakh and & 98.69 lakh, the ordered materials
was not supplied even after lapse of two to three years.

We further observed that the Company decided to exempt the suppliers from
payment of SD at the time of placing orders.

This not only defeats the objective of protecting Company’s financial interest
through assured supply of prescribed specifications but also resulted in extending
undue favour to suppliers

2.3.8.8 The first criteria/step to open quotations and tenders is submission of
Eamest Money Deposit by the bidders who are not exempted from such deposit
(firms registered as SSI units and central PSUs are exempted from deposit of
EMD). We observed in audit that in respect of four tenders (value ranging
between & 3.75 lakh and हैं 20.95 lakh), payment of EMD from bidders who were
not under the exempted category, was waived by the Company

We further observed that while the terms and conditions of tender document
provide for collection of EMD/SD/PBG, the Delegation of Powers (DOP) of the
Company provides powers to officers for approving waiver from payment. We are
of the opinion that the powers delegated were meant to be exercised for making
changes in the tender documents before inviting bids, as granting waiver after
receiving bids tantamount to extension of undue favour to suppliers. Since the
condition was incorporated to protect defects during the liability period, any such
waiver subsequent to calling and finalisation of tender is an undue favour and
hence waiver is unjustified. We also observed that this exemption is granted on
the grounds that the supplier is an Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM)
(including authorized dealers). We however noticed that even for consumables
materials like nuts, bolts, electrodes, caustic soda lye etc., SD is being waived
considering the supplier as OEM. Waiver of the condition to some bidders could
also invite litigations, when it is approved after invitation of tender.

During exit conference, the Government stated (October 2013) that the existing
system of waiver of SD would be reviewed. It was further stated that compliance
to instructions on collection of SD would be ensured in future.

71


Report on Public Sector Undertakings for the year ended 31 March 2013

2.3.9 Inordinate delay in issue of Material Receipt Certificate (MRCs)

2.3.9.1 MPSEB issued instructions in December 1996 to HODs at site to strictly
adhere to the time limit of seven days from the date of receipt of material

Company to obtain insurance policy/coverage against theft, burglary, fire
accidents and other perils of nature. Besides, it is also learnt that the total tangible

2.3.10 Non disposal of scrap/obsolete materials

2.3.10.1 Due to continuous operation of plants, wear and tear of
machinery/equipments and other material occurs, which on replacement were
scrapped and handed over by Return Indent (RI) to the concerned stores.

For disposal of scrap material the Company has entered into an agreement with
MSTC Limited? In this regard, guidelines were issued (January 2007) by MSTC
for preparation of lots for the purpose of e-auction, who acts on the basis of the
list of disposable materials received from its principal (Company). MSTC was
entitled for a service charge at the rate of three per cent on the sales realisation.

Scrutiny of records at Corporate Office, Jabalpur indicated that as at the end of
March 2013, scrap/obsolete items valued at हैं 4.66 crore (thermal- हैं 4.40 crore
and hydel - 0.26 crore) in 255 lots (thermal- 119 and hydel-136) was lying un-
auctioned in all the power houses.

The Management replied (October 2013) that except for 28 per cent of scrap
which were not purchased by buyers/not sold by the Company due to
unacceptable prices, the balance scrap was sold and lifted till October, 2013. The
undisposed scrap of 28 per cent valued at हैं. 1.22 crore included scrap valued at =
1.12 crore (scrap of हैं 0.26 crore lying undisposed since April 2010) at STPS,
Sarni

We found that the norms on time limits for publishing NITs and placing POs from
the receipt of indents from user departments were not fixed and there were several
instances of orders being placed after the close of the year for which requirement
was assessed. The Company (especially the plant management) had constantly
deviated its purchase procedures/policy by placing purchase orders based on
single tender basis. The Company had not also standardised the stocks levels to
avoid unnecessary holding of stocks. Many instances of inactive and non-moving
inventory were primarily due to unplanned/ unwarranted procurements resulting

° A Government of India Enterprise

74


Chapter-II-Review relating to Government Companies

in blocking of Company’s funds, besides coal losses beyond MPERC
norms/limits, excess inventory of secondary oil was also noticed. Materials/spares
were being received and inspected after completion of Annual overhauls for
which procurement was made and consequent retaining of spares for long periods
without use. The Company is exempting/relaxing the condition on recovery of
SD/EMD from its suppliers and as a result, the Company has not been able to bind
the supplier to financially indemnify in case of default/sub-standard supply and
lack of seriousness in supply ete.

Instances of inordinate delay in issue of MRCs, non-devolution of materials, non-
accountal of spares/re-usable items and other discrepancies in MAS were also
noticed.

In order to have an efficient material management the Company should:

> fix timeframe for assessment of requirement, inviting tenders and
awarding of purchase orders so as to achieve objective of planned
purchases and avoid delay in purchases;

>» consider fixing stock levels for different categories of stores and spares to
minimise inventory holding in excess of limits allowed by MPERC;

» introduce a mechanism to ensure that purchases by power stations are not
made on a single tender basis when items are not of proprietary nature;

> create sufficient infrastructure facilities for stacking and handling of coal
so as to arrest/minimise coal losses; and

> ensure compliance to its own policy/guidelines in collection/waiver of

security deposits for protecting its financial interest.

Introduction

Madhya Pradesh Road Development Corporation Limited, incorporated in July 2004 under
the Companies Act, 1956, is engaged in strengthening, upgradation and development of the
state highways and major district roads (MDR) in the State of Madhya Pradesh. The
Company ix mandated to provide an efficient, adequate and proper road infrastructure in
the state,

During 2009-10 to 2012-13, the Company completed 16 project highways under different
models of Build Operate and Transfer (BOT) Scheme under Public Private Partnership
(PPP).

Overview of executed rod projects

Out of the 16 completed projects, the Company completed 11 projects within the scheduled
period of completion. There were delays of 67 days to 564 days in achieving Commercial
Date of Operation in remaining five projects. The major reasons for delays were delay in
{financial closure, delay in handing over of site and problems associated with Right Of Way

Erroneous Treatment of Environmental Mitigation cost

The Company allowed 25 per cent escalation on the environment mitigation cost also
treating it as construction cost. This resulted in higher payment to concessionaries
amounting to रैं 62.77 lakh towards Viability Gap Funding.

Inerease in project cost due to adoption of higher specification

Considering the present and projected traffic on the road, two lane standard on the
Khimlasa-Malthon section of the Bina-Khimlasa-Malthon project highway was not
warranted. Developing this section on two lane standard thus resulted in unwarranted and
avoidable expenditure of 14.37 crore towards proportionate VGF

Payment of bonus and annuity under BOT (Toll plus Annuity) model

For the development of three project highways the Company formulated a new madel of
PPP viz, BOT (Toll plus Annuity). Due to not reducing the construction period as
recommended hy the Consultants payment of bonus far early completion resulted in
unintended henefit af €16.82 crare to the Concessionaires.

Selection of Public Sector undertaking as partner under PPP

Contrary to the principles af PPP the Campany selected a public sector entity for the
development of the Bina-Kurwai-Sironj Project highway.

16


Chapter-II-Review relating (o Government Companies

Incorrect fixation of toll

The Company did not formulate and document standard procedure for fixation of toll
rates. This resulted in unintended benefit to concessionaire to the tune of © 37.43 lakh
towards higher toll rates.

Delayed remittance of premium by the concessionaire

The concessionaire of the Indore-Ujjain project highway did not remit to the Company the
premium amounting to 27.69 crore as on 30 September 2013

2.4.1 Madhya Pradesh Road Development Corporation Limited, Bhopal (Company)
under the administrative control of Public Works Department, Government of
Madhya Pradesh was incorporated on 14 July 2004 as a wholly owned government
company under the Companies Act 1956, with the objective of executing roads and
allied infrastructure projects or for objects, as envisaged in public —private or joint
venture basis

The Company was notified (October 2005) as the State Highway Authority under
Madhya Pradesh Rajmarg Adhiniyam, 2004 and given mandate to develop and
operate State Highways. The Company is engayed in strengthening and development
of State Highways and Major District Road (MDR) projects. Majority of these are
on “Build Operate and Transfer (BOT) contracts under “Public Private Partnership"
(PPP) Model

The Company is required to follow guidelines and “Model Concession Agreement”
(MCA) of the Planning Commission, Government of India issued (between 2006 and
2008) for BOT contracts under PPP Model. The Company followed BOT (Toll)’,
BOT (Annuity)? and BOT (Toll p/us Annuity)’ models for contracts with
concessionaire* for construction and development of highways.

During the period from 2009-10 to 2012-13, the Company completed 16° project
highways at an estimated cost of € 3,803.81 crore® as detailed in Annexure 2.4.1.

Erroneous Treatment of Environmental Mitigation Cost

2.4.6.1 The Company appoints a technical consultant for each project highway on the
basis of tender and enters into an agreement for preparation of cost estimates for the
project. According to Clause 3.14 of model Request for Proposal for selection of
technical consultants, technical consultants would prepare cost estimates based on
indicative Bill of Quantities (BOQ) of various components. The consultant may add
25 per cent of such construction cost as a lump sum provision for meeting out any
physical and price contingencies, interest during construction, other financing cost
and pre-construction expenses etc

We noticed in respect of four out of 16 test checked project highways mentioned in
the Table No 2.4.1 that environmental mitigation cost’? was also included as
construction cost and 25 per cent contingencies were considered on such construction
cost. Whereas in respect of other project highways'®, the Company did not add
environment mitigation cost for providing contingencies of 25 per cent. Thus, the
Company did not follow a uniform practice for treating the environmental mitigation
cost.

This treatment of environmental mitigation cost had increased the total cost of the
four project highways and consequently increased the Viability Gap Funding to the
Concessionaire by € 62.71 lakh as detailed in the Table No 2.4.1.

Increase in project cost due to adoption of higher speci

2.4.6.2 The Company decided (June 2008) to take up Widening, Strengthening,
Maintaining and operating of Bina-Khimlasa-Malthon 37.63 km project highway on
two lane standard on BOT (Toll) basis. The project road consisted of two stretches of
road. One Stretch was a Major District Road (MDR) from Bina to Khimlasa
chowk"(BK) and the other was State Highway 42 from Khimlasa chowk to
Malthon'*(KM). The estimated project cost was & 69.11 crore.

The Company signed the concession agreement (CA) with concessionaire Essel Bina
Khimlasa Malthon Toll Road Limited on 7 August 2009 with a concession period of
25 years. Financial closure’? was achieved by the concessionaire on 25 May 2010.
Provisional completion certificate” for the road was issued by the Independent
Engineer in December 2011. Accordingly, the concessionaire commenced collection
of toll from December 2011.

We noticed that

‘The traffic as per 2008 estimates on BK section was 3722 PCL”! and on KM section
892 PCU. It was projected that the traffic on BK section would increase to 18722
PCU in the year 2034. In the same proportion, the traffic on KM section was
expected to increase to 4487 PCU. According to IRC64:1990”, for the traffic of
18000 PCU, the standard of the road would be “two lane plus paved shoulder” and for
the traffic of 4487 PCU, the standard would be “single lane”. So by the year 2034, the
BK section of the road would require a two lane plus paved shoulder standard,
whereas single lane standard would suffice for the KM section.

In spite of the vast difference in the traffic on the two sections of the road, for the
purpose of the project, the traffic on the BK section only was considered and both the
sections were developed on “two lane plus paved shoulder” standard.

Thus, the KM section of the road, where single lane standard would suffice even up to
the end of the concession period, was also developed on a two lane standard, thereby

“BK Section - length 17.04 Km

'S KM Scetion - length 20.59 Km

‘The Government replied (December 2013) that the standard and specifications of the
road was not compromised in view of present traffic. It was also stated that by
development of this project highway, the distance between Jhansi and Bhopal would
be reduced by 26 KMs and additional traffic was anticipated because of this reduced
distance. In the Exit Conference the Managing Director stated that the project
highway was declared as a State Highway

The reply is not acceptable as development of the KM section to a higher standard
was not in accordance with the norms of IRC in view of the low density of traffic.
Besides, if additional trattic was anticipated by the Company, the estimated increase
in collection of toll from additional traffic should have been considered and VGF
should have been reduced accordingly. Due to adoption of higher standard with
reference to IRC norms without justified reasons on record, the Public exchequer
suffered an additional burden of % 14.37 crore.

2.4.6.3 Execution of road projects

According to the concession agreements, the concessionaire was required to complete
the project in 24 months and for delays beyond the scheduled date of completion,
damages at the rate of 0.1 per cent of the Performance Security”® for each day of
delay was leviable. Compensation was also payable by the Company in the event of
delay in handing over the site/right of way to the concessionaire. The agreements
also prescribe time frame for completion of different activities of the project and the
concessionaire would be liable for damages for delay in adhering to the time frame
prescribed

Out of the 16 test checked project highways (13 completed and 3 ongoing projects),
we noticed delays in five completed projects. While in five completed works,
damages were levied as per agreement, in following two cases damages had not been
levied as and when due as discussed below:

> The work of strengthening and reconstruction of Bina-Kurwai-Sironj project
highway taken up under BOT (Toll) was awarded (November 2010) to
Telecommunications Consultants India Ltd, New Delhi (TCIL). There were
delays from the beginning in execution of the project. The Independent Engineer
reported (June 2012) that the Concessionaire had not achieved Project
Milestones” 1 and Il and stated that the Company might resort to claiming
damages in terms of the Concession Agreement, As of December 2012, only 17
per cent of the work was completed but the Company had not claimed damages
of & 73.90 lakh (up to April 2013) from TCLL.

After being pointed out (February 2013) by us, the Company called upon (April

2013) the concessionaire to deposit the damages. Progress of recovery is awaited
(January 2014)

2.4.6.4 The project highways Mandsaur-Pratapgarh, Sardarpur-Badnawar and

Model. The State Level Empowered Committee on Public Private Partnership
Projects decided (December 2010 and January 2011) that these project highways
would be taken up on equated fixed annuity as discovered through tender mechanism.

It may be seen trom the table no 2.4.2

> The Schedule completion period for these project highways was allowed 24
months in the concession agreement whereas Consultants suggested 12 months to
18 months as completion period. Reasons for allowing more time than that was
recommended by consultants were not on records made available, particularly
when payment of bonus for early completion was provided for in the agreement.
» The Concessionaires completed the project highways within six to nine months
against the scheduled completion period of 24 months
In cases of contracts involving payment of Annuity, Toll is not collected by the
concessionaire and he is allowed a Bonus for early completion as specified by the
Planning Commission in the model agreement. However in these cases, the
concessionaires were also to collect Toll. Since the concessionaire would collect
additional toll by achieving COD ahead of scheduled COD, he would any way
draw incremental revenue and such commencement of toll collection ahead of
scheduled date of COD would be incentive enough for early achievement of
COD. Thus, inclusion of the provisions relating to payment of Bonus for early
achievement of COD and not fixing the construction period as recommended by
the consultant, resulted in unintended benefit to the concessionaires to the tune of
% 16,82 crore in the form of Bonus.

The Government replied (December 2013) that (i) the Model Concession
‘Agreement (MCA) provides tor the construction period of two years and (ii)
payment of Bonus was as per the MCA, which was an incentive to the
concessionaire for completing the project speedily

v

The reply is not acceptable because the MCA for this type of PPP Model was not
in place and the Company could have fixed the construction period as
recommended by the consultant. By providing a longer construction period than
that was recommended by consultant, the Company had committed to pay bonus
for early completion to the extent of six to 12 months, even before
commencement of work.

Payment of Annuity

The project highways taken up under BOT (Toll) are eligible for a Viability Gap
Funding (VGF), which is usually restricted to 40 per cent of the cost of the project.
According to the note submitted (October 2010) by the Company to the Director,
Institutional Finance, GoMP, where the projects to be taken up under BOT (Toll plus
Shadow Fees”’) with a VGF of 40 per ceni, the quantum of the Shadow Fees was to
be restricted to 10 per cent of the project cost, so that VGF and Shadow Fee together
do not exceed 50 per cent of the project cost.

commitment to GoMP.

Selection of Public Sector undertaking as partner under PPP

2.4.6.5 According to the Secretariat for the Committee on Infrastructure, Government
of India, PPP means “an arrangement between a government or statutory entity on
one side and a private sector entity on the other, for the provision of public assets”.
Public private partnerships are a tool to bring together the strengths of both sectors. In
addition to maximising efficiencies and innovations of private enterprise, they
provide much needed capital to finance government programmes and projects,
thereby public funds could be available for core economic and social programs.

The Company signed (November 2010) concession agreement with
Telecommunications Consultants India Ltd, New Delhi (TCIL). TCIL being the best
bidder for the work of strengthening and reconstruction of two lane standard Bina-

Incorrect fication of toll

2.4.6.6 The State Government approves the levy of toll on the project highways being
developed under BOT scheme for various types of vehicles for a road or part thereof
(Homogeneous Section) from the date of COD issued by the Independent Engineer of
the project highway concerned. The rates of toll are fixed based on the length of the
road/Homogeneous Section on a “per trip per vehicle” basis

The toll is collected by the concessionaire. The toll rates are revised every year and

are computed on the basis of Wholesale Price Index (WPI) and are rounded off to the

nearest five rupees. The revised toll rates, based on the WPI for the year ending 31

March of the previous year, are made effective from 1 September every year”

We noticed that, there were no instructions or directions about the source of WPI.

Lack of such instructions resulted in fixation of higher toll rate as stated below:

> There was no uniformity in computation of toll for the year 2011-12"! in case of
four” project highways.

> In case of the project highways Labad-Manpur, Bina-Khimlasa-Malthon, and
Sagar-Damoh, the values for basic (WPI (A)) and WPI considered for the
relevant year (WPI (B)) were the “Monthly” values for the month of March 2007
and March 2011 respectively published by the office of the Economie Adviser,
Ministry of Commerce and Industry Government of India, instead of yearly value
of WPI as per State Government notification

> In case of the project highway Bhind-Mihona-Gopalpur, though the values of
WPI adopted were “yearly”, however, these were based on the base year 1993-94
instead of latest WPI available for base year 2004-05

This resulted in fixation of higher toll rate and consequently higher collection of toll
by the concessionaire from the users. Such collection of higher toll during the period
from September 2011 to August 2012 on these four project highways worked out to हैं
37.43 lakh as detailed in Annexure-2.4.5

The Government stated (December 2013) that though the values of WPI (A) and WPI
(B) were being adopted as was prescribed for some time, it was realised that the value
of WPI (B) might be considered as the average of the WPI from April to March of
that particular year. It was further stated that from September 2012, the WPI with

Delayed remittance of premium by the concessionaire
2.4.6.7 The Company decided to take up the work of “Widening, Upgradation and

Strengthening” of the existing two lane Ujjain-Indore project highway to four lane
standards under BOT (Toll).

The Company signed concession agreement with Mahakaleshwar Tollways Private
Limited (concessionaire) on 17 September 2008 and financial closure was achieved
by the concessionaire on 26 May 2009. As per clause 26.2.1 of the concession
agreement, the concessionaire was to pay a Premium of 26.38 per cent of the total toll
revenue in first week of the following month, which would increase at the rate of one
per cent per annum. Independent Engineer issued provisional completion certificate
on 15 November 2010 and the concessionaire commenced toll collection from that
date.

We noticed (February 2013) that the concessionaire had paid the premium to the
Company up to March 2012. For the year 2012-13 and 2013-147 the concessionaire
collected & 26.76 crore towards toll but had not deposited (September 2013) the
amount of premium of € 7.69 crore™* with the Company on the grounds of insufficient
collection of toll due to the non payment of toll by certain users and not getting
support {rom Administration for toll collection.

The Management stated (April 2013) that the collection of toll by the Concessionaire
was less than his financial obligation. It further stated that legal opinion on non-
payment was being sought. In the Exit Conference, the Managing Director explained
that all the revenues generated through toll were deposited by the concessionaire in an
escrow account. From this account, by way of a waterfall arrangement, the monies
were appropriated towards operation and maintenance of the project highway, toll
collection expenses, interest, other expenses of the SPV and only after this, it could be
appropriated towards premium and lastly to the concessionaire.

The reply is not specific as to how the Company proposed to ensure that the premium
due to the Company in terms of concession agreement was realised on time

after the monsoon, preferably in the months of April and October every year and that
the concessionaire shall ensure that at no point during the operation period, the
roughness in the road index shall exceed 3000 mm per km and 1000 mm in any 200
m length.
